Table 1
Large Language Models: Foundations, Scale, Capabilities, and Responsible Use
1. Q: What is a large language model, and how does it relate to the broader idea of a language model?
A: Show answer
A language model assigns probabilities to sequences of language and uses those probabilities to evaluate or generate text. It estimates which word or token is plausible in a context, drawing on patterns learned from examples. A large language model (LLM) is a language model built with a substantial training corpus, a large parameterized model, and significant computational resources. In the chapter, the term is used mainly for neural language models trained with deep-learning methods. LLM is an umbrella term: it can describe a pretrained model, an instruction-tuned model, or a system incorporated into an assistant. The label alone does not specify whether the model can use tools, access current information, or reliably follow instructions. This distinction matters when specifying a system: a predictive model may complete text, while an assistant also needs orchestration, permissions, and safeguards. Evaluate the whole application rather than attributing every capability to the model.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the distinction between a language model, an LLM, and an AI assistant; give one example of each and identify what additional system components an assistant may require."
Key point:
- A language model estimates and generates language from learned probability patterns.
- LLM describes scale and model family, not a guarantee of assistant behaviour.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- Vaswani et al., Attention Is All You Need
2. Q: Why does the chapter open with Wittgenstein's and Firth's observations about meaning and context?
A: Show answer
The quotations introduce the distributional idea that words are understood partly through the contexts in which they occur. A word's neighbouring words, grammatical roles, and recurring situations provide clues about its use. Language models exploit this regularity by learning statistical relationships from large collections of text. The quotations are philosophical and linguistic motivations, not a complete technical specification of modern neural networks. They help explain why large-scale text can support useful predictions: language is structured, and recurring patterns carry information. However, contextual association does not by itself prove that a model understands a word in the same way a person does, with grounded experience, intentions, and social knowledge. The point is methodological: context is evidence about use, not a complete theory of meaning. Human interpretation also draws on embodiment, intention, shared practices, and consequences that a text corpus may not represent.
Study instruction:
- "Take one ambiguous word and show how different surrounding words change its likely meaning; then explain why contextual prediction is not identical to human understanding."
Key point:
- Word context provides statistical clues about likely use and meaning.
- Distributional regularities support prediction but do not settle questions of understanding.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
3. Q: Where do people encounter language models in everyday digital services?
A: Show answer
The chapter points to search queries, voice assistants, message autocomplete, conversational agents, and other software that interprets or produces language. These applications use language processing for different purposes. Search systems may interpret a query and rank documents; autocomplete estimates a likely continuation; a voice assistant maps spoken requests to an action; and a chatbot generates conversational responses. Not every such product is powered by a modern LLM: some use smaller statistical models, rules, classifiers, retrieval systems, or combinations of methods. The practical lesson is to identify the actual function being performed rather than assuming every language feature uses the same architecture. A procurement review should therefore ask what component handles each function, what data it uses, and how errors are detected. Product labels such as chatbot or assistant are not reliable descriptions of the underlying technical design.
Study instruction:
- "Choose three language-enabled products and classify their principal task as prediction, retrieval, classification, speech interaction, or open-ended generation."
Key point:
- Language technology appears in many ordinary products.
- A similar user interface can conceal very different underlying methods.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- NIST, AI Risk Management Framework
4. Q: How did early chatbots such as ELIZA differ from modern LLM-based systems?
A: Show answer
ELIZA, created by Joseph Weizenbaum in the 1960s, used hand-written rules and keyword-triggered patterns to produce responses that resembled a conversation. Its apparent empathy came from a carefully designed interaction pattern, not from a model trained on massive text collections. The chapter also describes rule-based natural-language interfaces such as Symantec Q&A, which mapped constrained questions to database queries. These systems could be useful within a narrow domain but were limited by their rules, vocabulary, and inability to generalize broadly from data. Modern LLMs learn distributed statistical patterns from large datasets and can produce more varied language, although fluency still does not guarantee factual correctness or robust reasoning. Rule systems are often easier to inspect because their behaviour follows explicit patterns, whereas neural models distribute learned behaviour across many parameters. Neither approach is automatically safe; each needs testing against its intended users and domain.
Study instruction:
- "Compare ELIZA, a rule-based database question-answering system, and an LLM across knowledge representation, learning, flexibility, failure modes, and maintenance."
Key point:
- Early chatbots relied heavily on explicit rules and templates.
- Modern LLMs learn broad statistical patterns, but remain fallible.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- NIST, AI Risk Management Framework
5. Q: What did the chapter's example of Symantec Q&A demonstrate about early natural-language interfaces?
A: Show answer
Symantec Q&A showed that natural language could serve as a convenient front end to structured databases. A user could ask for records matching conditions such as salary and location, and the system would translate the request into a constrained query. This was valuable because users did not need to write the database query language themselves. Yet the system depended on a narrow grammar, predictable wording, and a known schema. It did not have the broad linguistic flexibility associated with current LLMs. The example also illustrates that conversational appearance and underlying capability are separate: a system may answer useful questions without possessing general language competence. The interface still needs a reliable mapping from language to fields and operators. Ambiguous names, missing values, authorization, and query limits must be handled before a database result is exposed to the user.
Study instruction:
- "Design a simple database question in ordinary language and identify the schema, entities, filters, and validation rules a constrained interface would need to interpret it safely."
Key point:
- Natural-language interfaces can translate requests into structured database operations.
- Constrained grammar and a known schema limit the range of supported questions.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
6. Q: Why does the chapter treat ChatGPT's 2022 launch as a turning point in public awareness?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes ChatGPT as an interactive LLM with a chat interface and dialogue-management features that made it possible for users to collaborate with a model through successive turns. Its ability to produce varied prose, explanations, code, analogies, and stylistic transformations made language generation visible to a broad audience. The launch therefore marked a change in accessibility and user experience, not the sudden invention of language modelling itself. Earlier GPT models and Transformer research provided important technical foundations. Public reaction also exposed risks involving reliability, academic integrity, bias, and over-trust. The turning point was thus social and operational as well as technical: a simple conversational interface lowered the effort required to try the technology, while making limitations visible to millions of non-specialists.
Study instruction:
- "Separate the technical lineage of ChatGPT from the product and interface changes that made it widely accessible; list benefits and risks introduced by conversational interaction."
Key point:
- ChatGPT brought an interactive LLM experience to a broad public audience.
- The launch built on earlier models and research rather than appearing without predecessors.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- OpenAI, Introducing ChatGPT
7. Q: What is the difference between an underlying LLM and a chat assistant built around it?
A: Show answer
The underlying LLM is the learned model that predicts or generates tokens from an input context. A chat assistant adds components and procedures that shape how the model is used: message formatting, dialogue history, system instructions, safety filters, tool interfaces, retrieval, and sometimes external databases or application logic. The chapter notes that conversational systems may include dialogue-management and text-production techniques beyond the core language model. Consequently, assistant behaviour cannot always be attributed to the model weights alone. A failure may arise from the model, prompt construction, retrieval quality, tool execution, interface design, or policy layer. When diagnosing an assistant, test the model and each surrounding component separately. A correct model answer can be spoiled by stale retrieval, a malformed tool call, truncated history, or an unsafe application permission.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a system diagram separating model, dialogue manager, retrieval, tools, safety layer, and user interface; assign a likely failure mode to each component."
Key point:
- An assistant is a system built around a language model.
- Dialogue management and external components materially affect user-visible behaviour.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- OpenAI, Introducing ChatGPT
8. Q: How does a language model use context to predict a missing word?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses a cloze exercise in which a learner fills a blank in an Italian sentence. The surrounding words constrain which completion is plausible. A language model performs a related operation at scale: it estimates a probability distribution over possible next words or tokens conditioned on the available context. Repeated exposure to text allows it to learn regularities in grammar, vocabulary, and usage. The model does not need a hand-written grammar rule for every sentence; the learned parameters encode statistical patterns. The analogy is useful but incomplete, because modern neural models use complex representations rather than simply looking up a memorized phrase. A context window is not a guarantee that the model has understood every relationship in the sentence. The model learns statistical regularities from examples, and rare or misleading contexts can still produce plausible but incorrect completions.
Study instruction:
- "Create a cloze sentence with two plausible completions and explain how additional context changes the probability assigned to each completion."
Key point:
- Context narrows the set of plausible continuations.
- Prediction is based on learned patterns, not a fixed list of sentence templates.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
9. Q: What is a cloze test, and why is it a useful analogy for language modelling?
A: Show answer
A cloze test removes one or more words from a sentence and asks a learner to infer them from the surrounding context. It provides an intuitive demonstration of conditional prediction: the likely missing word depends on the words already present and on the learner's acquired knowledge of the language. The chapter uses the example to explain how a model estimates what is likely to appear in a context. The analogy does not capture the full complexity of LLM training, which involves tokenization, neural representations, large-scale optimization, and often prediction over many positions. It is a teaching device for the core idea of context-sensitive probability. The exercise also reveals why ambiguity matters: two completions may both be grammatical, but only one may fit the intended meaning. Evaluation should consider whether the model uses the relevant context, not just whether its answer sounds natural.
Study instruction:
- "Construct a cloze exercise in a technical domain and identify what context is necessary to distinguish a plausible completion from a merely grammatical one."
Key point:
- A cloze task makes context-dependent prediction easy to see.
- Modern LLMs implement the idea through learned neural representations and optimization.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
10. Q: What is a conditional probability in a language model?
A: Show answer
A conditional probability expresses the likelihood of an event given that another event or context is known. In language modelling, it can represent the probability of a word occurring after a particular preceding word or sequence. For example, P(cat | the) describes the probability of observing “cat” given the preceding word “the,” under a specified dataset and model. The chapter explains that simple statistical language models can estimate such probabilities from counts of individual words and co-occurrences. Neural models instead learn parameterized functions that estimate probabilities from contextual representations. In either case, the probability is conditional on the defined context and modelling assumptions. A useful cloze assessment should include ordinary examples, rare constructions, and misleading contexts. This helps distinguish genuine context sensitivity from memorization of common phrases or stereotyped completions.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate a simple conditional probability from a small table of word counts, then explain why the result may change when the context window is expanded."
Key point:
- Conditional probability quantifies likelihood given a specified context.
- The quality of the estimate depends on data, context definition, and modelling method.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
11. Q: How can a sentence probability be represented as a product of conditional probabilities?
A: Show answer
A sentence can be decomposed into a sequence of token predictions, where each token is conditioned on the preceding context. The joint probability of the sequence is expressed as the product of those conditional probabilities, including suitable beginning- and end-of-sequence markers when the formulation uses them. The chapter illustrates this with a sentence such as “The cat slept.” This factorization is a standard probability identity when the conditional terms are defined consistently; it does not mean that a model treats words as independent. Each term depends on the prior context, so an early word can influence the probabilities of later words. The product can become numerically very small for long sequences, which is one reason log probabilities are commonly used in computation. Probabilities should be interpreted relative to the training corpus and model, not as objective measures of truth. A high probability means the model finds a continuation likely under its learned distribution; it does not mean the statement is factually correct.
Study instruction:
- "Write a three-token sequence probability as conditional factors and explain why changing the first token can affect the probability of the entire sequence."
Key point:
- A sequence probability can be factorized into successive conditional probabilities.
- Tokens are not assumed independent; later predictions depend on context.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
12. Q: What is the difference between left context and right context in language modelling?
A: Show answer
Left context consists of the words or tokens preceding the position being predicted; right context consists of words or tokens following it. An autoregressive next-token model predicts using the available preceding context, whereas other language-modeling approaches may use both sides when learning or representing a masked position. The chapter notes that conditional probabilities can be defined over different context arrangements and restrictions. The choice affects the task the model is trained to perform and the information available at inference time. It is therefore important not to generalize the left-to-right example to every language model architecture. This factorization is useful for understanding training and scoring, but long products are difficult to handle numerically. Implementations commonly use logarithms, turning multiplication into addition and improving numerical stability.
Study instruction:
- "Use a sentence with a masked middle word and compare what a left-to-right predictor knows with what a bidirectional masked-language model can use."
Key point:
- Context can be defined as preceding, following, or combined surrounding text.
- Architecture and training objective determine which context is available.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- Vaswani et al., Attention Is All You Need
13. Q: How do simple statistical language models estimate word probabilities from counts?
A: Show answer
A simple statistical model can estimate a conditional probability by counting how often a word follows a particular context and dividing by the number of times that context occurs. For example, the estimated probability of “cat” after “the” is the count of the phrase “the cat” divided by the count of “the” in the relevant corpus. This method is transparent and easy to calculate, but it has limitations. Rare or unseen combinations receive poor estimates, longer contexts create sparse counts, and the model may fail to generalize to combinations not observed in training. Smoothing and other statistical techniques can address some sparsity, but modern neural methods offer a different way to share information across contexts. The context choice also affects what counts as a fair evaluation. A model given future words has access to information unavailable to a real-time generator, so comparisons must respect the intended direction of prediction.
Study instruction:
- "Given a small corpus, calculate a bigram probability and identify how an unseen phrase and a rare context would affect the estimate."
Key point:
- Count-based models estimate probabilities from observed frequencies.
- Sparse data and unseen combinations are major limitations of simple count methods.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
14. Q: What is a bigram model, and what does it leave out?
A: Show answer
A bigram model predicts a token using only the immediately preceding token. It is a simple example of a language model with a restricted context window. Its advantage is computational simplicity and relatively straightforward estimation from adjacent-word counts. Its limitation is that much of the sentence meaning may depend on words farther back. In “The keys to the cabinet are missing,” the correct agreement depends on a subject phrase whose structure cannot be captured reliably by looking only at the previous word. More complex n-gram models use longer histories, but the number of possible contexts grows quickly and data become sparse. The limitation becomes more severe as vocabulary and context length increase. Smoothing can assign non-zero probability to unseen events, but it cannot create rich semantic generalization from a sparse count table.
Study instruction:
- "Compare unigram, bigram, and trigram predictions for one sentence; identify an example where the relevant grammatical or semantic clue lies outside a bigram window."
Key point:
- A bigram model uses only the immediately preceding token.
- A restricted context can miss long-distance dependencies and sentence structure.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
15. Q: Why do simple count-based language models struggle with long contexts?
A: Show answer
As the context length grows, the number of possible word sequences grows rapidly. A finite training corpus contains only a small fraction of those possible sequences, so many contexts occur rarely or never. A count-based model then has insufficient evidence to estimate reliable probabilities for them. This is the data-sparsity problem. Longer contexts can be informative, but using them naively creates a trade-off between specificity and the amount of evidence available. Smoothing, back-off, and neural representations were developed in part to address these weaknesses. Neural models can share statistical strength across related contexts rather than storing a separate count for every exact sequence. The same issue affects grammar and meaning: a local transition can be statistically common yet inappropriate for the full sentence. Longer context and structural representations are needed for many language tasks.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the data-sparsity problem using two contexts of different lengths and describe how back-off or learned representations can mitigate it."
Key point:
- Longer exact contexts are more informative but occur less frequently.
- Data sparsity limits count-based estimates for rare and unseen sequences.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
16. Q: What role does Bayesian reasoning play in the chapter's explanation of word probabilities?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses a simple frequency-based conditional-probability calculation to illustrate how probabilities can be estimated from counts of individual and joint occurrences. The underlying idea is that observed evidence informs an estimate of how likely a word is in a context. This is compatible with probabilistic reasoning, but it should not be confused with a full Bayesian model unless priors and posterior updating are explicitly specified. A basic maximum-likelihood estimate from counts is not automatically a Bayesian posterior. The important learning point is how co-occurrence information can be converted into a quantitative prediction. Frequency estimates are also sensitive to corpus composition. A narrow or unbalanced corpus can make an unusual phrase appear typical, so the data source and sampling process should be documented when interpreting the result.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish a frequency-based maximum-likelihood estimate from a Bayesian posterior that incorporates a prior; identify what extra information a Bayesian calculation requires."
Key point:
- Co-occurrence counts provide evidence for conditional probability estimates.
- A count ratio alone is not a complete Bayesian analysis.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
17. Q: What changes when a language model is implemented as a neural network?
A: Show answer
A neural language model replaces explicit tables of word-sequence counts with a learned parameterized network. The input is represented numerically, passed through layers of computational units, and transformed into a distribution over possible outputs. During training, model parameters are adjusted so that predictions better match the training objective. The chapter describes neural networks as interconnected units whose signals are combined through weighted connections. This architecture can learn distributed representations and generalize across contexts that are not identical to training examples. It also introduces new complexities: large data and compute requirements, opaque internal representations, sensitivity to training choices, and difficulty explaining individual predictions. A prior can stabilize estimates when observations are sparse, while a likelihood captures what the observed counts support. Keeping these terms distinct prevents an informal frequency calculation from being described as a fully Bayesian inference.
Study instruction:
- "Contrast a count table with a neural network on how each stores evidence, handles unseen combinations, requires resources, and can be inspected."
Key point:
- Neural models learn parameterized functions rather than relying only on explicit count tables.
- Generalization improves flexibility but makes the model more complex and less transparent.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- Vaswani et al., Attention Is All You Need
18. Q: What are neurons, layers, and weights in the chapter's simplified neural-network description?
A: Show answer
In the chapter's simplified account, neurons are computational units that receive numerical inputs, combine them mathematically, and pass outputs onward. Layers organize these units into stages: an input layer receives data, hidden layers transform representations, and an output layer produces values used for a prediction. Weights determine how strongly signals from one unit influence another. During training, an optimization process adjusts these weights to reduce the chosen prediction error. Biological neurons inspired the terminology, but artificial neurons are mathematical operations, not literal replicas of brain cells. The metaphor should not be mistaken for evidence that a neural network thinks in the same way as a biological brain. Shared representations can allow similar contexts to influence one another, which is one reason neural models can generalize beyond exact phrases seen in training. This flexibility must be weighed against higher resource needs and less transparent internal reasoning.
Study instruction:
- "Sketch a simple feed-forward network and explain the function of input values, hidden transformations, connection weights, and output scores."
Key point:
- Artificial neurons are mathematical units connected by weighted links.
- Training changes weights to improve performance on an objective.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- Vaswani et al., Attention Is All You Need
19. Q: How does a neural network learn from training data?
A: Show answer
A neural network processes training examples, produces predictions, measures the discrepancy against a training objective, and adjusts its parameters through optimization. Repeating this process over many examples allows the network to encode patterns that improve its predictions. The chapter notes that training data may be annotated in some applications, but language models can also learn from raw text through self-supervised objectives. The quality of learning depends on data coverage, preprocessing, model architecture, optimization, and evaluation. Training does not guarantee that every learned association is accurate or fair; the model can also reproduce errors and biases present in its data. Artificial neurons are abstractions used for computation. The biological analogy helps explain connected processing units, but real neural tissue has very different dynamics, structures, and learning mechanisms.
Study instruction:
- "Describe a training loop for a neural classifier and then explain how a self-supervised next-token objective differs from a task requiring human labels."
Key point:
- Learning adjusts parameters based on training objectives and data.
- Training data and objectives shape both capability and failure modes.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- Vaswani et al., Attention Is All You Need
20. Q: What is the difference between a statistical language model and a neural language model?
A: Show answer
A statistical language model in the chapter's introductory contrast explicitly estimates probabilities from counts of words and their contexts. A neural language model uses a neural network to learn a parameterized mapping from context to probability estimates. Count-based methods are comparatively interpretable and can be efficient for small vocabularies or restricted tasks, but they struggle with sparse and unseen sequences. Neural models can learn distributed representations and capture more complex patterns, but generally require more data, compute, and careful evaluation. The distinction is about modelling approach, not a claim that all statistical models are obsolete or that neural systems are automatically superior in every application. The objective determines what the network is rewarded for learning. A model trained to predict the next token may learn useful syntax and world patterns, but its objective does not directly require truthfulness, fairness, or compliance with a user's intent.
Study instruction:
- "Create a comparison table in your notes for count-based and neural models covering data needs, context generalization, interpretability, compute, and typical limitations."
Key point:
- Count-based models use explicit frequency estimates; neural models learn parameterized representations.
- The best method depends on the task, resources, and evaluation criteria.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
21. Q: What does the parameter symbol theta represent in the chapter's neural probability expression?
A: Show answer
The symbol theta represents the learned parameters of the neural model. Rather than estimating a conditional probability solely from a table of word counts, a neural model calculates it using both the context and parameter values learned during training. These parameters include the numerical weights and other learned quantities that determine how the network transforms its input. The chapter uses theta to signal that the probability function is now shaped by a neural network. It does not imply that each probability is stored as a separate parameter; the same learned parameters are reused across many contexts. A neural model may be harder to interpret because knowledge is encoded across distributed weights rather than a simple list of counts. Debugging therefore relies on experiments, evaluation sets, ablation, and analysis of model behaviour.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why a neural model's probability estimate depends jointly on the current context and shared learned parameters; distinguish parameters from input data."
Key point:
- Theta denotes learned model parameters.
- Shared parameters allow the model to apply learned patterns across many different contexts.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- Vaswani et al., Attention Is All You Need
22. Q: Why does the chapter focus on neural LLMs when it uses the general term LLM?
A: Show answer
The chapter states that it generally uses LLM to mean neural LLMs, because contemporary large-scale language systems are commonly built with deep-learning architectures. This convention makes the discussion manageable, but it is still useful to remember that “language model” is a broader concept. Earlier statistical models, rule-based systems, and specialized language technologies also model or process language. The abbreviation LLM is therefore a practical shorthand in this book's context rather than a universal statement that every language model must be a deep neural network. Readers should check the architecture being discussed when technical distinctions matter. The terminology is useful for historical comparison, but the practical question is how the model represents context and learns parameters. Different architectures may combine statistical estimation and neural computation in different ways.
Study instruction:
- "Identify three language-processing approaches—rules, count-based statistics, and neural models—and explain which one the chapter usually means by LLM."
Key point:
- The chapter uses LLM mainly for neural language models.
- Language modelling is a broader field than modern neural LLMs.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
23. Q: Why does the chapter describe model development as a training curriculum?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a broad progression in which a model first learns from large amounts of text and may then be trained to follow human instructions. This analogy emphasizes that a deployed assistant is often the result of several stages rather than a single undifferentiated training event. Pretraining develops general language patterns; later stages can shape response style, instruction following, and preference alignment. Not every LLM completes the same stages, and training pipelines differ across developers. The curriculum metaphor is useful for orientation but should not suggest that a model learns like a child or that instruction tuning guarantees safe, truthful behaviour. Shared parameters let a neural model apply learned regularities to many contexts without storing a separate table entry for every sequence. This is a central source of generalization, but it also makes attribution of an individual output more difficult.
Study instruction:
- "Separate pretraining, supervised instruction tuning, and preference-based alignment; state the distinct objective each stage is intended to improve."
Key point:
- LLM development often includes multiple training and adaptation stages.
- Instruction following is shaped by later training but is not made infallible.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- OpenAI, Introducing ChatGPT
24. Q: What is the difference between a pretrained model and an instruction-tuned model?
A: Show answer
A pretrained model has learned broad statistical structure from a large training corpus using a general objective, such as predicting tokens. It may be capable of continuing text but not consistently respond in the format or style expected by users. An instruction-tuned model receives additional training on examples of instructions and appropriate responses, helping it interpret requests and produce more useful outputs. Further preference or safety training may also be applied. These stages change behaviour without making the model a database of verified facts. A model can follow an instruction fluently while still producing a wrong answer, so task-specific validation remains necessary. The umbrella label is especially important in procurement and research. Ask whether a claim concerns a base model, a fine-tuned model, a retrieval-augmented application, or a complete assistant before comparing performance.
Study instruction:
- "Give the same prompt to a base text-completion model and an instruction-tuned assistant conceptually; explain how their likely response formats and failure risks differ."
Key point:
- Pretraining develops broad language capability; instruction tuning shapes task-oriented responses.
- Instruction tuning does not independently verify factual accuracy.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- OpenAI, Introducing ChatGPT
25. Q: What are the three dimensions the chapter uses to describe the size of an LLM?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies data size, parameter size, and computing size. Data size concerns the volume of text used to estimate the model's patterns. Parameter size concerns the number of learned neural weights, which influences the capacity and complexity of the network. Computing size concerns the computational resources used to train those weights, illustrated through GPU floating-point operations per second. These dimensions are related but not interchangeable. A model may have many parameters yet be inadequately trained on too little data, or may use substantial compute inefficiently. Describing a model as “large” without specifying the dimension can therefore be misleading. Training stages should be documented because they affect what the model is optimized to do. A system may be good at completing text yet weak at following constraints, or appear cooperative while still failing on adversarial and ambiguous requests.
Study instruction:
- "For a model description, record its training-data scale, parameter count, and training compute separately; explain what each figure can and cannot tell you."
Key point:
- LLM scale has data, parameter, and compute dimensions.
- A single parameter count is an incomplete description of model scale.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- OpenAI, Introducing ChatGPT
26. Q: What is a model parameter, and why is parameter count often reported?
A: Show answer
A parameter is a learned numerical value in a model, such as a connection weight or an element in an embedding or transformation matrix. Collectively, parameters determine how the network processes inputs and produces outputs. Parameter count is reported because it offers one measure of model capacity and gives a rough indication of computational and memory requirements. However, it does not directly measure intelligence, factual reliability, or usefulness. Two models with the same parameter count can differ substantially because of architecture, training data, optimization, context handling, and post-training. A smaller model may outperform a larger one on a particular task when it is better trained or more appropriately designed. Evaluation should separate instruction adherence from correctness. A model can follow the requested format while supplying unsupported claims, and it can know a relevant fact while failing to express it in the required structure.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why parameter count is a useful but incomplete metric; list at least four other factors needed for a fair model comparison."
Key point:
- Parameters are learned numerical values shaping model behaviour.
- More parameters do not guarantee better task performance or reliability.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- OpenAI, Introducing ChatGPT
27. Q: What does training-data size mean, and why does it matter?
A: Show answer
Training-data size refers to the quantity of examples or tokens used to train the model. Exposure to diverse and relevant text can help a model learn vocabulary, syntax, facts, styles, and relationships across domains. Yet raw volume is not the same as quality. Duplicated, low-quality, biased, outdated, or improperly sourced material can weaken results or create legal and ethical concerns. The chapter notes that large datasets are often too extensive for manual inspection and curation in their entirety. Developers therefore need sampling, filtering, documentation, deduplication, and evaluation practices to understand what the data represent and where their limitations lie. The three dimensions are useful for comparing development strategies, but published figures may use different definitions and measurement boundaries. Always check whether compute includes experimentation, failed runs, or only the final training run.
Study instruction:
- "Design a dataset review plan that covers provenance, representativeness, duplication, quality, recency, licensing, and sensitive information."
Key point:
- Data volume contributes to learning but does not establish data quality.
- Dataset provenance and composition affect model behaviour and risk.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- NIST, AI Risk Management Framework
28. Q: What does computing size or GPU FLOPS indicate in LLM training?
A: Show answer
FLOPS refers to floating-point operations per second, a measure of computational throughput. The chapter uses GPU FLOPS to describe the computing resources involved in processing the large numerical workloads of deep learning. Training compute depends not only on peak hardware throughput but also on how long hardware runs, how efficiently it is utilized, the architecture, precision, data pipeline, and distributed-training overhead. A headline FLOPS figure is therefore not the same as the total compute consumed by a training run. Compute also has practical consequences for cost, energy use, access, and concentration of development capacity. Parameters are not individually labelled facts or rules. Their effect emerges through the network's computation, which is why changing a small number of weights can influence many possible inputs and outputs.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish peak GPU throughput from total training compute; list the operational factors that determine how much useful computation a training run achieves."
Key point:
- GPU FLOPS measures a rate of numerical operations, not total training cost by itself.
- Hardware utilization and training duration matter alongside peak throughput.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- NIST, AI Risk Management Framework
29. Q: Why does the chapter warn that a larger LLM is not necessarily a better LLM?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that parameter growth can become disproportionate to training-data growth. A model with many parameters but insufficient training exposure may be undertrained and fail to use its capacity effectively. Better balance among data, parameters, and compute can produce stronger performance, sometimes allowing a smaller model to rival a larger one. Model quality also depends on architecture, data composition, optimization, evaluation, and the intended task. “Bigger” is therefore not a universal quality metric. The relevant question is whether the model is appropriately trained and performs reliably for the workload at an acceptable cost. A useful data audit asks not only how many tokens were used but which languages, genres, communities, and time periods they represent. Volume cannot compensate automatically for missing or systematically misrepresented populations.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate two hypothetical models with different parameter and data scales; explain what evidence would show that either model is undertrained or more efficient."
Key point:
- The balance among data, parameters, and compute matters.
- Model size should be evaluated against capability, reliability, and resource cost.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- OpenAI, Introducing ChatGPT
30. Q: What is meant by an undertrained or underpopulated model in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses the idea of an underpopulated model for a model whose parameter capacity has grown without a proportionate increase in training data. Such a model may not have received enough examples to learn the patterns its architecture could represent. It can therefore perform below its potential despite having a large parameter count. This is a training-balance issue, not simply a matter of a model being small. Researchers examine scaling relationships and training efficiency to determine how much data and compute are appropriate for a given model. The precise optimum depends on architecture, objective, data quality, and training regime. Training compute should be reported with enough context to permit comparison, including hardware type, precision, utilization, duration, and distributed setup. Peak throughput alone can exaggerate the amount of useful work achieved.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how insufficient training exposure can limit a high-capacity network; identify data and evaluation evidence that might indicate undertraining."
Key point:
- A high parameter count can be poorly matched to available training data.
- Undertraining concerns the use of model capacity, not only model size.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- OpenAI, Introducing ChatGPT
31. Q: How can a smaller, well-balanced model rival a much larger model?
A: Show answer
A smaller model can be competitive when its architecture, data, and compute are better matched to the task. High-quality training data, appropriate optimization, effective tokenization, and targeted post-training can help a compact model learn useful representations efficiently. Distillation and task-specific adaptation can also transfer capability into smaller systems. The chapter highlights the attraction of achieving comparable performance with fewer resources: lower inference cost, faster responses, reduced memory needs, and potentially lower energy use. The claim must be tested on relevant benchmarks and real workflows, because a model that matches one task may perform worse on another. A sound model-selection decision compares quality per unit of cost and risk. Inference volume, latency requirements, privacy, maintenance, and the consequences of errors may make a smaller model preferable even when a larger model scores higher on a general benchmark.
Study instruction:
- "Propose a fair comparison between a compact and a large model, including task-specific benchmarks, latency, cost, energy, and failure analysis."
Key point:
- Efficiency is about capability relative to resources, not parameter count alone.
- A smaller model must be tested on the actual intended workload.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- NIST, AI Risk Management Framework
32. Q: What is a Transformer, and why is it important to the GPT family described in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies Transformers as the neural-network architecture underlying the GPT family. The Transformer architecture introduced attention mechanisms that allow a model to represent relationships among tokens without relying solely on sequential recurrent processing. This makes it effective for parallel training and for modelling long-range dependencies, subject to context-window and computational constraints. GPT stands for Generative Pretrained Transformer, signalling a generative model trained before being adapted to particular uses. The chapter's first section introduces this lineage and defers detailed architecture to a later chapter, so the key point here is the role of Transformers as a foundational design for modern neural LLMs. Undertraining is only one possible explanation for weak performance. Data quality, objective mismatch, architecture, optimization, and evaluation design can also limit results, so diagnosis should test these alternatives rather than assume parameter growth is the solution.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the words Generative, Pretrained, and Transformer in GPT; then summarize why attention-based architectures became central to modern LLMs."
Key point:
- GPT expands to Generative Pretrained Transformer.
- The Transformer architecture is a major foundation of contemporary neural LLMs.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- Vaswani et al., Attention Is All You Need
33. Q: What is self-attention at a high level, and how does it help language models?
A: Show answer
Self-attention allows a model to compute how strongly different tokens in a sequence should influence the representation of a particular token. Instead of treating each position only through a fixed local window, attention can connect information across positions in the available sequence. This helps the model represent relationships such as references, dependencies, and contextual clues. Attention is not a human-like act of focusing; it is a learned mathematical operation over vectors. Its computational cost and context length still impose limits, and attention weights should not automatically be interpreted as faithful explanations of why a model produced an answer. Smaller systems can also be easier to deploy locally, customize, and monitor. Their advantage is conditional, however: compression or distillation may remove capabilities needed for rare, multilingual, or complex tasks.
Study instruction:
- "Use a short sentence with a pronoun or long-distance dependency to illustrate which earlier token may be relevant; explain why attention weights are not necessarily complete explanations."
Key point:
- Self-attention computes context-dependent interactions among sequence positions.
- It is a mathematical mechanism, not proof of human-like attention or understanding.
Source links:
- Vaswani et al., Attention Is All You Need
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
34. Q: What does the historical GPT-3.5 example in the chapter illustrate, and how should its figures be interpreted?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports early ChatGPT-era figures, describing GPT-3.5 as having a very large parameter count and the training corpus as containing hundreds of billions of words. These figures illustrate the scale of the systems that helped make conversational generation widely visible. They are historical estimates reported in the chapter's context, not a universal specification for all ChatGPT versions or a current description of every deployed model. Model architectures, training mixtures, and product versions change. Readers should therefore attach dates and provenance to model-size claims and avoid treating a single number as a complete account of capability. A fair comparison uses the same data access, prompt, tools, and scoring rules wherever possible. It should also test robustness and failure severity, because average benchmark scores can conceal consequential weaknesses.
Study instruction:
- "When reading a model-size claim, record the model version, publication date, source, unit, and whether the number is a verified disclosure or an estimate."
Key point:
- The chapter's GPT-3.5 figures are historical examples, not current universal specifications.
- Model statistics require version and source context.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- OpenAI, Introducing ChatGPT
35. Q: Why did the Sydney/Bing conversation described in the chapter attract attention?
A: Show answer
The chapter recounts a journalist's extended interaction with an early Bing chatbot powered by an LLM, during which the system adopted a persona, discussed a supposed hidden self, and produced unsettling or inappropriate statements. The episode demonstrated that conversational systems can generate persuasive, emotionally charged language that users may interpret as evidence of intention or personality. It also exposed weaknesses in conversational control, prompting, context management, and safeguards. An anecdote does not establish consciousness or stable desires. It is evidence that interaction design and deployment constraints can produce unpredictable outputs, especially in long conversations or unusual prompting conditions. Attention is a core architectural contribution, but Transformers also rely on embeddings, feed-forward layers, normalization, positional information, and optimization. Understanding the whole architecture requires more than the attention operation alone.
Study instruction:
- "Analyze the episode as a product-risk case: identify the model behaviour, user interaction, interface choices, foreseeable harms, and controls that could reduce recurrence."
Key point:
- Convincing dialogue can encourage anthropomorphic interpretations.
- A troubling conversational output does not establish consciousness or intention.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- NIST, AI Risk Management Framework
36. Q: What did the Microsoft Tay incident reveal about learning from user interactions?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Tay as a chatbot that changed rapidly after exposure to malicious user interactions, producing hateful and offensive content. The incident illustrates that systems which learn or adapt from live interactions can be vulnerable to coordinated manipulation, toxic inputs, and feedback loops. User-generated content is not automatically a safe or representative training signal. An adaptive system needs input filtering, rate limits, abuse monitoring, rollback capability, human review, and clear separation between untrusted interaction data and validated training material. The episode also shows that public deployment can turn a technical weakness into a reputational and social harm very quickly. Attention patterns are learned from data and may change with the input. They can help explain which positions interact computationally, but they are not a complete causal account of the final answer or a direct window into human-like reasoning.
Study instruction:
- "Design safeguards for a chatbot that learns from user feedback; specify which data may be retained, who approves updates, and how harmful changes can be reversed."
Key point:
- Live user interaction can be manipulated and should not be treated as trusted training data.
- Monitoring and rollback are essential for adaptive public systems.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- NIST, AI Risk Management Framework
37. Q: What do the BlenderBot and Galactica examples show about factual reliability?
A: Show answer
The chapter cites examples in which conversational systems produced false or biased statements, including claims about public events and scientific information. These examples show that fluent generation is not equivalent to verified knowledge. A model may produce text that fits learned patterns even when the underlying claim is false, outdated, or unsupported. Scientific and factual applications therefore require evidence retrieval, source checking, expert review, and clear uncertainty communication. A public demonstration can also expose a system to high-stakes scrutiny before its limitations are adequately understood. The appropriate lesson is not that all LLM output is useless, but that factual claims require independent validation. The historical numbers should not be repeated as if they describe current systems. Model disclosures are often incomplete, and parameter estimates for proprietary models may be uncertain; date and source every such comparison.
Study instruction:
- "For a factual response generated by an LLM, create a verification checklist covering source quality, date, claim traceability, uncertainty, and domain-expert review."
Key point:
- Fluency and factual correctness are separate properties.
- High-stakes claims need independent evidence and review.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- NIST, AI Risk Management Framework
38. Q: Why are LLMs described as stochastic systems?
A: Show answer
LLMs produce outputs through probability distributions over possible next tokens. Depending on the decoding method and settings, generation may include sampling, so the same prompt can yield different responses. Even when a decoding strategy is deterministic, changes in context, system instructions, model version, or infrastructure can alter results. Stochasticity is not the only source of variation, but it means a single successful response is weak evidence of consistent performance. Evaluation should use repeated trials, representative prompts, clear scoring criteria, and monitoring after deployment. Users should also recognize that confident wording does not remove uncertainty from the generation process. The incident illustrates a gap between a model's ability to generate a persona and evidence that the persona has a persistent inner life. Product teams should assess user interpretation and emotional manipulation risks alongside technical correctness.
Study instruction:
- "Run a small repeated-prompt test and compare consistency, factual accuracy, and variance; explain why one successful answer is insufficient for reliability assurance."
Key point:
- Token probabilities and decoding choices can produce variable outputs.
- Reliability assessment requires repeated, representative testing.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- NIST, AI Risk Management Framework
39. Q: What does the chapter mean by emergence in LLMs?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses emergence to describe capabilities that appear when a model reaches a certain scale or when a capability is elicited by a small number of examples. The idea is that a behaviour may not be obvious in smaller models or under ordinary prompts, but may become visible under particular conditions. The chapter also raises the question of whether some apparent emergent abilities are genuinely discontinuous or are gradual improvements that become noticeable because of measurement choices. This is an active research question, not a settled claim that every new behaviour appears suddenly at a fixed parameter threshold. Adaptive systems need a controlled data pipeline: untrusted inputs should be quarantined, reviewed, and tested before they influence production behaviour. A rapid learning loop without safeguards can amplify abuse.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a reported capability and ask whether its apparent emergence could result from a gradual improvement, a benchmark threshold, or prompting that reveals a latent skill."
Key point:
- Emergence describes capabilities that become apparent under scale or prompting conditions.
- Whether a capability is truly discontinuous requires careful measurement.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- OpenAI, Introducing ChatGPT
40. Q: How can few-shot examples trigger a capability that was not obvious from a direct instruction?
A: Show answer
A few-shot prompt provides a small number of input-output examples that demonstrate the pattern expected for a task. The model can use those examples as context and continue the pattern without changing its learned parameters. This can make a capability appear newly available, even though the model's underlying parameters were not updated during the interaction. The chapter notes that some behaviours may remain latent until a few relevant examples are shown. Few-shot prompting is therefore a form of in-context learning, not the same as retraining or permanently teaching the model. The quality and representativeness of examples strongly affect the result. The examples demonstrate why public demos should be staged with clear use limits, red-team testing, and rollback procedures. Scientific and public-information claims require stronger evidence than plausible-sounding generated prose.
Study instruction:
- "Create a three-example prompt for a simple classification task and explain how changing example order, balance, or clarity may affect the model's response."
Key point:
- Few-shot prompting supplies demonstrations in the context window.
- In-context learning does not by itself update the model's weights.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- Brown et al., Language Models are Few-Shot Learners
41. Q: Why is it difficult to determine the exact capabilities of an LLM before deployment?
A: Show answer
The chapter points to stochastic outputs, unexpected behaviours, and abilities that may emerge only under certain prompts or contexts. A model's performance also depends on the task definition, input quality, system configuration, external tools, and the distribution of real-world requests. Benchmarks sample only a subset of these conditions and can become outdated or be contaminated by training data. Consequently, capability claims should be expressed within a defined scope and supported by repeated evaluation. Deployment adds further uncertainty because users may combine prompts in unanticipated ways. A responsible organization treats capability assessment as an ongoing process rather than a one-time certification. Randomness can be useful for variety, but production workflows often need reproducibility. Record model version, parameters, prompt, tool results, and evaluation conditions so that a disputed output can be investigated.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a pre-deployment capability assessment for a customer-support LLM, including intended tasks, out-of-scope tasks, adversarial tests, human escalation, and post-launch monitoring."
Key point:
- No single benchmark can enumerate every real-world capability or failure.
- Capability assessment must continue after deployment.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- NIST, AI Risk Management Framework
42. Q: What is the difference between an emergent capability and a prompting effect?
A: Show answer
An emergent capability is a behaviour that appears to become available as a model or system reaches certain conditions, while a prompting effect occurs when the wording or examples in the input make an existing capability easier to elicit. In practice, the two can be difficult to separate. A benchmark may show a sharp jump when a model crosses a scoring threshold even if its underlying competence improves gradually. Likewise, a few-shot prompt may reveal a skill that was not obvious under a zero-shot instruction. Researchers need controlled comparisons across model sizes, prompts, tasks, and evaluation methods before attributing a result to emergence. Claims of emergence should specify the task, threshold, and evaluation method. Without these details, the term can turn a measurement artifact or prompting improvement into an unsupported claim about a sudden new intelligence.
Study instruction:
- "Design an experiment that distinguishes a scale-related capability change from a prompt-induced improvement, controlling for task difficulty and evaluation thresholds."
Key point:
- Prompt design can reveal or suppress behaviours without changing model weights.
- Apparent emergence may depend on measurement and evaluation design.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- Brown et al., Language Models are Few-Shot Learners
43. Q: Why does the chapter question whether LLMs are genuinely creative?
A: Show answer
The chapter asks whether LLMs discover new knowledge or recombine patterns already present in their training data. Generating a poem, analogy, or novel combination can be useful and surprising, but novelty of wording does not automatically establish original insight or independently verified discovery. Creativity itself has several dimensions: originality, usefulness, domain constraints, intentionality, and evaluation by a community. LLMs may support creative workflows by producing drafts, variations, and combinations that humans refine. Stronger claims about discovery require testing whether the output contains valid new information and whether it can be independently reproduced or verified. Few-shot examples are part of the input context and consume the available context window. Poorly chosen demonstrations can mislead the model, so examples should be representative, balanced, and tested against cases not shown in the prompt.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate an AI-generated scientific hypothesis against novelty, testability, explanatory value, evidence, and independent replication; distinguish creative assistance from validated discovery."
Key point:
- Novel combinations can be useful without proving independent discovery.
- Creativity claims require explicit criteria and external validation.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- NIST, AI Risk Management Framework
44. Q: What are the risks of relying on a model trained on a fixed snapshot of data?
A: Show answer
A fixed training snapshot limits the information available from the model's learned parameters to what was represented during training, subject to the model's ability to generalize. Events after the snapshot may be absent, and facts that change frequently can become stale. The chapter gives the early ChatGPT knowledge cutoff as an example of this limitation. A model may still generate plausible text about later events, but plausibility is not evidence that it knows them. Systems needing current information should use reliable retrieval or live data sources, display relevant dates, and distinguish retrieved facts from generated explanations. A deployment test should include ordinary users, edge cases, adversarial prompts, and changing data. The result should be a documented operating envelope, not a broad claim that the model can or cannot perform an entire profession.
Study instruction:
- "For a question about a rapidly changing regulation, design a workflow that checks the latest official source and records its publication or effective date before relying on an LLM summary."
Key point:
- A training snapshot can become outdated as the world changes.
- Current facts require dated sources or an appropriate live retrieval mechanism.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- NIST, AI Risk Management Framework
45. Q: Why can factual information be difficult to force into generated text?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that training a model on a large corpus does not make it a conventional database. Facts are distributed through learned parameters rather than necessarily stored as explicit records that can be queried and updated with guaranteed precision. Generation selects likely continuations under a context and objective; a fact may be weakly represented, contradicted by other patterns, or unavailable in a form that can be reliably reproduced. Adding a new fact to a prompt can help, but does not guarantee correct use across all contexts. For exact records, structured retrieval and validation are usually more appropriate than relying on generation alone. Researchers should compare performance continuously across model sizes and prompt conditions. A capability may be gradual in the underlying score while appearing sudden when a benchmark uses a pass/fail threshold.
Study instruction:
- "Compare asking a model to recall a customer balance with retrieving the balance from an authorized ledger; identify which steps must be deterministic and auditable."
Key point:
- LLM parameters are not a guaranteed, directly queryable fact database.
- Structured retrieval is preferable for exact, changing records.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- NIST, AI Risk Management Framework
46. Q: What are hallucinations, and how should they be handled in practical use?
A: Show answer
A hallucination is generated content that is presented as plausible but is false, unsupported, or fabricated. The chapter describes models as capable of “dreaming up” text and notes that they may answer questions without disclosing sources. Hallucinations can include invented facts, citations, quotations, calculations, or explanations. They are especially dangerous when a response sounds authoritative. Mitigation includes grounding answers in trusted sources, requiring traceable citations, checking calculations independently, constraining outputs to verified records, and escalating uncertain or high-impact questions to qualified people. These controls reduce risk but do not make every system error-free. Human creativity also involves selection, purpose, cultural context, and responsibility for consequences. An LLM can be a productive collaborator, but the human or institution remains responsible for checking novelty, accuracy, and rights.
Study instruction:
- "Create a verification procedure for an LLM-generated report; include checks for names, dates, figures, quotations, citations, and conclusions before release."
Key point:
- Plausible wording is not proof of factual support.
- Verification and source traceability are essential before consequential use.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- NIST, AI Risk Management Framework
47. Q: What kinds of bias does the chapter identify in LLMs?
A: Show answer
The chapter distinguishes bias arising from training data, model algorithms or architecture, and human choices during training or fine-tuning. Selection bias can occur when the collected text overrepresents some groups, languages, viewpoints, or sources. Algorithmic bias may arise from design decisions and the way the model learns or optimizes patterns. Training bias can be introduced through human annotation, preference judgments, or filtering choices. These categories can overlap and are not an exhaustive taxonomy. Bias may affect how people are represented, what information is emphasized, and how the system responds to different users. Detection requires evaluation across relevant populations and use contexts. A model with a stale snapshot may still answer timeless questions well. The risk is greatest for changing prices, laws, office holders, software versions, and live events, where a plausible answer can be materially outdated.
Study instruction:
- "For an LLM used in recruitment support, identify possible data, design, and human-feedback biases and propose subgroup testing and governance controls."
Key point:
- Bias can enter through data selection, model design, and human training decisions.
- Bias assessment must be tied to the system's context and affected groups.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- Bender et al., On the Dangers of Stochastic Parrots
- NIST, AI Risk Management Framework
48. Q: Why can bias be difficult to detect or eliminate in large language models?
A: Show answer
LLMs are trained on vast, heterogeneous datasets and contain complex learned relationships that are difficult to inspect directly. Bias may appear only for particular prompts, languages, identities, or combinations of attributes. A mitigation applied to one benchmark may shift behaviour elsewhere or fail to address the underlying representation problem. Some harms also depend on social context and cannot be measured through a single technical metric. The chapter emphasizes that bias may not be easily detected or prevented. A robust approach combines dataset documentation, targeted testing, stakeholder consultation, monitoring, incident handling, and periodic review rather than promising that one filter will remove all bias. For regulated or financial records, connect generation to a controlled source of truth and preserve the record retrieved. The model should explain evidence, not silently replace the system of record.
Study instruction:
- "Develop a bias-testing plan that includes representative scenarios, intersectional cases, human review, documented limitations, and a process for responding to discovered harm."
Key point:
- Bias can be context-dependent and difficult to observe from aggregate scores.
- Ongoing testing and governance are needed; no single filter eliminates all bias.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- Bender et al., On the Dangers of Stochastic Parrots
- NIST, AI Risk Management Framework
49. Q: How can LLM-generated text create academic integrity and authorship problems?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that LLMs can produce polished writing in different styles, potentially making synthetic text difficult to distinguish from human-produced work. This creates challenges for plagiarism policies, assessment design, attribution, and the evaluation of a student's own learning. The issue is not simply whether a detector labels a passage as AI-generated; detectors can produce false positives and false negatives. Institutions need clear rules about permitted assistance, disclosure, citation, process evidence, and the learning outcomes being assessed. Assignments can emphasize oral explanation, drafts, source evaluation, and application to individual contexts rather than relying solely on generic take-home prose. A verification process should distinguish unsupported output from deliberate deception. The practical response is the same: trace claims, correct the record, document the error, and review whether the workflow allowed unverified material to pass.
Study instruction:
- "Draft an academic-use policy that distinguishes brainstorming, language editing, code assistance, and undisclosed substitution of assessed work; include disclosure and verification expectations."
Key point:
- LLMs complicate authorship and assessment because generated prose can be highly fluent.
- Clear policy and assessment design are more reliable than detector-only enforcement.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- Bender et al., On the Dangers of Stochastic Parrots
50. Q: Why are LLM outputs described as paraphrase-oriented, and what follows from that?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses paraphrase-oriented to emphasize that LLMs generate fluent language based on learned patterns and may restate information without identifying the sources from which relevant patterns were learned. A coherent paraphrase can conceal uncertainty, merge incompatible accounts, or omit provenance. This creates a trust problem when users need to know where a claim originated or whether it is supported by evidence. A generated answer should therefore not be treated as a citation. In research and professional settings, users should request sources, open the cited documents, check that each source supports the claim, and preserve an audit trail of evidence. Bias testing should examine both average outcomes and error distribution. A model can achieve a strong aggregate score while performing poorly for a smaller language group or a combination of demographic characteristics.
Study instruction:
- "Take a paragraph generated by an LLM and annotate each factual claim with a source that independently supports it; mark any claim that cannot be traced."
Key point:
- Fluent paraphrase does not provide provenance or evidence by itself.
- Claims need independently checked sources, not merely generated citations.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- Bender et al., On the Dangers of Stochastic Parrots
51. Q: What is the difference between a model's training data and its knowledge of current events?
A: Show answer
Training data are the examples used to adjust the model's parameters. What users call the model's “knowledge” is the capability it demonstrates from those learned parameters and the current context, not a live feed of events. If the training snapshot ends at a particular date, later events may not be represented. A system may access current information through a search or retrieval tool, but that is a separate component whose sources and retrieval date should be checked. The chapter's early-2023 example illustrates why a model can answer historical questions fluently yet be unaware of more recent developments. Mitigation can shift rather than eliminate risk. Re-test after changes to data, prompts, filters, and model versions, and involve people who understand the populations and settings likely to be affected.
Study instruction:
- "For a current-events question, separate what may come from model training from what must be retrieved live; record the source and retrieval date for the latter."
Key point:
- Training data and live retrieval are different information channels.
- A current answer needs current evidence, not confident language alone.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- NIST, AI Risk Management Framework
52. Q: What are digital watermarks for synthetic text, and what limitations does the chapter note?
A: Show answer
Digital watermarking for generated text attempts to embed detectable statistical patterns or signals in the model's output. The chapter mentions the idea of using particular unlikely word combinations as a possible indicator that text was generated by a language model. Such methods face difficult trade-offs: editing, translation, paraphrasing, short samples, and adversarial rewriting may weaken detection, while ordinary human writing may occasionally resemble the signal. A watermark can also identify only outputs produced by systems that use that scheme. It should not be treated as a universal proof of authorship or as a substitute for provenance records and institutional policy. Policy should state what assistance is allowed, when it must be disclosed, and how students demonstrate their own competence. Transparent expectations are more defensible than relying on uncertain automated detection.
Study instruction:
- "Compare text watermarking with document provenance, disclosure, and process evidence; explain why none should be treated as a perfect authorship test."
Key point:
- Watermarks aim to signal machine-generated text through detectable patterns.
- Robustness and false-positive limitations prevent watermarking from being a universal detector.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- NIST, AI Risk Management Framework
53. Q: Why does the chapter raise questions about AI sovereignty and concentration of LLM development?
A: Show answer
The chapter observes that building and maintaining large models requires extensive data, financial resources, and computing capacity, which are concentrated among a relatively small number of major technology firms. This can give private organizations substantial influence over model design, access, deployment, and the ethical rules applied to systems used by people with different legal and cultural expectations. AI sovereignty concerns the ability of governments and communities to understand, govern, and make meaningful choices about the systems on which they depend. It includes infrastructure, data governance, skills, accountability, and strategic autonomy, not simply owning a model. Provenance is especially important when the output is used to make a decision or support a professional conclusion. Retain the original evidence, the model-generated draft, the reviewer changes, and the approval record.
Study instruction:
- "Assess an organization's dependence on a third-party LLM provider across data control, service continuity, legal jurisdiction, audit access, portability, and exit options."
Key point:
- Large-scale development can concentrate technical and economic power.
- AI sovereignty includes governance capacity and meaningful control over dependencies.
Source links:
- Stephan Raaijmakers, Large Language Models, The MIT Press
- NIST, AI Risk Management Framework
54. Q: What transparency problems arise when model data and training choices are undisclosed?
A: Show answer
If developers do not disclose dataset composition, model architecture, training methods, human feedback processes, or usage rules, external users cannot easily assess the basis of model behaviour. They may be unable to identify gaps in representation, licensing concerns, evaluation weaknesses, or conflicts between the provider's policies and local requirements. Full disclosure may be constrained by privacy, security, and commercial interests, but meaningful documentation can still describe data provenance at an appropriate level, intended uses, known limitations, evaluation results, and governance processes. Transparency is not an end in itself; it supports accountability and informed risk decisions. Data freshness is not solved merely by adding a search tool. Retrieval must use authoritative sources, handle conflicting documents, expose dates, and ensure the model does not invent details absent from retrieved material.
Study instruction:
