2. Q: What did McCulloch and Pitts contribute to the foundations of neural networks?
A: Show answer
In 1943, Warren McCulloch and Walter Pitts proposed a mathematical model of a neuron whose activity could be represented through logical operations. Their simplified unit used all-or-none signals and showed how networks of such units could implement structured computations. This was not a modern trainable neural network or a language model. Its importance was conceptual: it connected neuroscience, logic, and computation by showing that networks of simple neuron-like units could process information. Later researchers added learning rules, richer architectures, and numerical representations, but the idea that useful computation can emerge from connected simple units remains foundational.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how a threshold-like artificial neuron can represent a logical decision, then identify two biological details the model omits."
Key point:
- The 1943 model connected neural activity with formal computation.
- It was a conceptual foundation, not a modern learning system.
Source links:
-
- McCulloch and Pitts (1943), A Logical Calculus of the Ideas Immanent in Nervous Activity
3. Q: How did Rosenblatt's perceptron extend the earlier artificial-neuron idea?
A: Show answer
Frank Rosenblatt's perceptron introduced a system whose parameters could be adjusted using labelled examples. Rather than specifying every decision manually, a training procedure changed weights in response to prediction errors. Early demonstrations included image-recognition tasks in which sensor readings represented features and the model learned to distinguish classes. The perceptron therefore made learning from data a practical part of the architecture. It remained limited in the patterns it could represent, but it established an influential approach: numerical inputs, adjustable weights, a decision rule, and a feedback signal based on known answers.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a fixed logical neuron with a perceptron trained on labelled examples, focusing on what the training procedure changes."
Key point:
- The perceptron introduced adjustable weights learned from examples.
- Its learning ability was important, although its representational limits remained.
Source links:
-
- Rosenblatt (1958), The Perceptron: A Probabilistic Model for Information Storage and Organization in the Brain
4. Q: What are the main components of a basic perceptron?
A: Show answer
A perceptron receives numerical inputs, multiplies each input by an adjustable weight, combines the weighted values, and applies a decision rule to produce an output. In the chapter's example, photosensors provide measurements from an image. Each weight controls how strongly a sensor contributes to the combined score. A threshold then converts that score into a class decision, such as 0 or 1. The perceptron does not understand an image as a person does; it learns a mathematical boundary in the supplied feature space. The quality of the result depends on the features, labelled examples, and whether the chosen architecture can represent the required boundary.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the path from sensor readings through weights and summation to the final binary classification."
Key point:
- Inputs describe an example numerically and weights control their influence.
- A threshold converts the combined score into a class decision.
Source links:
-
- Rosenblatt (1958), The Perceptron: A Probabilistic Model for Information Storage and Organization in the Brain
5. Q: What are features, labels, and training examples in perceptron learning?
A: Show answer
A feature is a numerical attribute used to describe an example. In an image task, features may represent sensor responses to edges, colours, or positions. A label is the target class supplied for that example, such as whether the image belongs to a particular category. A training example pairs its feature vector with its known label. The perceptron compares its prediction with the label and adjusts weights to reduce the error. Learning depends on whether the features capture distinctions relevant to the task and whether the examples represent cases the model will encounter. Labels provide the teaching signal; they do not explain the underlying meaning of the class.
Study instruction:
- "Construct a small labelled dataset with three features per example and explain what each feature and label represents."
Key point:
- Features encode measurable characteristics of examples.
- Labels provide target outputs for supervised learning.
Source links:
-
- Rosenblatt (1958), The Perceptron: A Probabilistic Model for Information Storage and Organization in the Brain
6. Q: How do weights and a threshold determine a perceptron's classification?
A: Show answer
Each input is multiplied by its corresponding weight, and the weighted values are combined into a score. The threshold rule maps that score to a class. A larger positive weight makes an input contribute more strongly in the positive direction, while a negative weight can reduce the score. Changing the weights changes the location and orientation of the decision boundary. The chapter's diagram uses a constant and threshold comparison to illustrate the mechanism; implementations may express the threshold through a bias term or an equivalent parameter. The key point is that a basic perceptron learns a linear decision rule in its feature space rather than memorizing every training example.
Study instruction:
- "Use a two-feature example to show how changing one weight can move a decision boundary and change a prediction."
Key point:
- Weights control each feature's contribution to the score.
- The threshold turns a numerical score into a discrete class.
Source links:
-
- Rosenblatt (1958), The Perceptron: A Probabilistic Model for Information Storage and Organization in the Brain
7. Q: How does the perceptron learning rule update its weights?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a rule that adjusts a weight using the difference between the true label and the perceptron's prediction, multiplied by the corresponding feature value and a learning-rate parameter. In simplified notation, the new weight equals the previous weight plus learning rate times prediction error times feature value. If the prediction is correct, the error term may be zero and no correction is needed for that example. If it is wrong, the feature values determine how individual weights are adjusted. The process repeats across examples and training cycles. It is supervised learning because the model needs known labels to calculate the error signal.
Study instruction:
- "Work through one numerical update using a true label, predicted label, feature value, and learning rate."
Key point:
- Weight updates use prediction error to correct the model.
- Feature values determine how individual weights respond to that error.
Source links:
-
- Rosenblatt (1958), The Perceptron: A Probabilistic Model for Information Storage and Organization in the Brain
8. Q: What is the learning rate, and why does it matter?
A: Show answer
The learning rate controls the size of each weight adjustment. A larger value produces more aggressive changes and may speed early learning, but it can also cause overshooting or unstable updates. A smaller value makes changes more cautious but may require many iterations and leave the model learning too slowly. The appropriate value depends on the data and training procedure. The chapter introduces the learning rate as a parameter that must be set. In larger neural networks, optimizers and learning-rate schedules provide more elaborate ways to control parameter updates, but the underlying trade-off between step size and stability remains important.
Study instruction:
- "Describe what could happen if the learning rate is extremely high or extremely low during training."
Key point:
- The learning rate scales each parameter update.
- Fast updates are not necessarily better; stability and convergence matter.
Source links:
-
- Rosenblatt (1958), The Perceptron: A Probabilistic Model for Information Storage and Organization in the Brain
9. Q: What does it mean for a classification problem to be linearly separable?
A: Show answer
A dataset is linearly separable when a single straight line in two dimensions, or a hyperplane in higher dimensions, can divide the classes without misclassifying the training examples. A basic perceptron can learn such a boundary under the relevant training conditions. The boundary is determined by a weighted combination of input features and a threshold. If examples from different classes are interwoven so that no single linear boundary separates them, a basic perceptron cannot represent the required classification perfectly. Linear separability is therefore a property of the geometry of the labelled data, not simply a measure of how much data the model has seen.
Study instruction:
- "Plot a two-feature dataset and determine whether one straight line can separate its classes."
Key point:
- A single-layer perceptron produces a linear decision boundary.
- The geometry of the data determines whether that boundary is sufficient.
Source links:
-
- MIT Press, Minsky and Papert, Perceptrons
10. Q: Why is the XOR problem a classic limitation of a single-layer perceptron?
A: Show answer
Exclusive OR returns true when exactly one of two binary inputs is true and false when both have the same truth value. When the four input combinations are plotted, the two positive cases occupy opposite corners and the negative cases occupy the other corners. No single straight line can separate the positive points from the negative points. A basic single-layer perceptron therefore cannot learn XOR perfectly, regardless of how its weights are adjusted. This example isolates a representational limitation: the problem is not merely poor training or too few examples. The architecture lacks the nonlinear decision structure required for the pattern.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the four XOR points, label their classes, and test whether a single straight line can separate them."
Key point:
- XOR is not linearly separable.
- The single-layer perceptron's limitation is architectural, not simply a lack of training data.
Source links:
-
- MIT Press, Minsky and Papert, Perceptrons
11. Q: What did Minsky and Papert's 1969 analysis reveal about perceptrons?
A: Show answer
Marvin Minsky and Seymour Papert examined the computational capabilities of perceptrons and demonstrated important limitations for particular problem classes, including XOR for a basic single-layer perceptron. Their work challenged exaggerated expectations that early perceptrons would automatically solve general pattern-recognition problems. The chapter notes historical debate about how widely researchers appreciated that hidden layers could overcome some of these limitations. It would be inaccurate to say the book proved that all neural networks were incapable of learning complex patterns. Its analysis concerned defined perceptron architectures and showed why richer structures were needed.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish the limitation established for a basic perceptron from the capabilities of later multilayer networks."
Key point:
- Minsky and Papert identified real limitations of single-layer perceptrons.
- Their analysis should not be generalized into a claim that all neural networks fail.
Source links:
-
- MIT Press, Minsky and Papert, Perceptrons
12. Q: How do multilayer perceptrons overcome the basic XOR limitation?
A: Show answer
A multilayer perceptron adds one or more hidden layers between the input and output layers. Hidden units transform the original inputs into intermediate representations. When the units use nonlinear activation functions, the network can combine these transformations to create decision regions that a single linear unit cannot express. For XOR, hidden units can form intermediate distinctions that allow the output layer to separate the positive cases from the negative ones. Additional layers do not guarantee that every problem will be easy to learn, but they expand the family of functions the network can represent. This is the key move from one linear boundary to richer nonlinear computation.
Study instruction:
- "Sketch a network with two inputs, a hidden layer, and an output, then explain how hidden units can represent XOR."
Key point:
- Hidden layers create intermediate representations.
- Nonlinear transformations allow networks to model patterns beyond one linear boundary.
Source links:
-
- MIT Press, Minsky and Papert, Perceptrons
13. Q: What do hidden layers contribute to deep learning?
A: Show answer
Hidden layers transform information between input and output. Each layer receives representations from the previous layer, applies learned weights and nonlinear operations, and passes a new representation onward. Stacking layers allows the network to build increasingly complex combinations of features. In image tasks, early layers may respond to simpler patterns while later layers combine them into more abstract distinctions; the exact structure depends on training and architecture. The chapter emphasizes that networks can contain many hidden units and layers, but there is no universally precise numerical threshold at which a network becomes deep. The term describes a family of multilayer methods rather than a fixed count.
Study instruction:
- "Trace how a representation can change across three hidden layers and explain why each layer needs a useful nonlinearity."
Key point:
- Hidden layers create intermediate representations.
- Depth permits repeated transformations that can express complex relationships.
Source links:
-
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
14. Q: Why are nonlinear activation functions important in multilayer networks?
A: Show answer
If every layer performed only a linear transformation, stacking many layers would still be equivalent to one overall linear transformation. The network would not gain the ability to represent nonlinear relationships such as XOR. Activation functions introduce nonlinear responses after weighted combinations, allowing successive layers to reshape the representation space. The chapter describes neurons as applying nonlinear transformations to incoming signals. The choice of activation function affects training behavior and the kinds of transformations that are easy to learn. Nonlinearity is therefore not a cosmetic addition; it is a core reason deeper networks are more expressive than a single linear layer.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why stacking linear layers remains linear, then show where a nonlinear activation changes the network's expressive power."
Key point:
- Nonlinear activations increase the expressive power of multilayer networks.
- Depth without nonlinearity does not provide the same representational benefit.
Source links:
-
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
15. Q: What does the term deep learning mean in the chapter?
A: Show answer
Deep learning refers to neural-network methods that use multiple layers of learned transformations. The chapter explains that multilayer networks were conceived earlier, but their practical impact expanded with advances in computing, data, and training methods. It also cautions that there is no universally precise boundary separating a deep network from a shallow one. The word deep is therefore a descriptive label for architectures with substantial internal layered structure, not a strict layer count that applies to every field. The important idea is that successive transformations can build complex representations from raw inputs and support tasks such as language processing.
Study instruction:
- "Describe a network with several hidden layers and explain why deep is descriptive rather than a universal numerical threshold."
Key point:
- Deep learning uses multilayer neural networks.
- The chapter does not define one layer count that makes every network deep.
Source links:
-
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
16. Q: What is backpropagation, and why can deep networks be difficult to train?
A: Show answer
Backpropagation calculates how changes in network parameters contribute to prediction error and propagates gradient information through the network so weights can be updated. In a deep network, the error signal must pass through many layers. The chapter explains that gradients could become very small as they travelled backward, leaving early layers with weak correction signals. This is known as the vanishing-gradient problem and can make learning slow or ineffective. Modern methods use architectural and optimization techniques to mitigate it, but it is inaccurate to say the issue has disappeared in every network or setting. Training depth requires attention to gradient flow as well as representational capacity.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how a small gradient passed through many layers can limit learning in the earliest layers."
Key point:
- Backpropagation communicates error information to earlier layers.
- Vanishing gradients can weaken learning in deep networks.
Source links:
-
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
17. Q: Why does the chapter connect temporal networks with language processing?
A: Show answer
Language is sequential: a word's role and meaning often depend on words that came before or after it. A model processing text therefore needs a way to represent order and context over successive positions. The chapter describes temporal neural networks as systems that use information from earlier sequence elements when making later predictions. In text, sequence position can be viewed as progression through a one-dimensional stream. Backpropagation can train such models, although long dependency paths create optimization challenges. This history helps explain why architectures that preserve and combine context are central to language modelling.
Study instruction:
- "Take one sentence and show how the interpretation of a later word depends on earlier words in the sequence."
Key point:
- Language modelling must account for context across a sequence.
- Sequence processing makes temporal information and long-range dependencies important.
Source links:
-
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
18. Q: Why must natural-language processing convert text into numerical representations?
A: Show answer
Computers operate on encoded data, so language-processing algorithms need numerical representations of words, tokens, sentences, or documents. A useful representation should do more than assign an identifier: it should preserve information that helps a model relate linguistic units. If two words occur in similar contexts or have related meanings, their representations should make those relationships available to the algorithm. The chapter moves through increasingly informative approaches, from arbitrary integer identifiers to vector spaces, TF-IDF, and learned embeddings. Each method solves some problems while leaving others unresolved, motivating the development of contextual representations used by modern LLMs.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a short sentence and describe what information a numerical representation should preserve for a language model."
Key point:
- Text must be encoded numerically before standard neural algorithms can process it.
- Representation quality affects how much linguistic context a model can exploit.

- scikit-learn User Guide, Feature Extraction and TF-IDF
19. Q: Why are arbitrary integer IDs inadequate as semantic representations of words?
A: Show answer
Assigning a unique integer to each word distinguishes word identities, but the numbers themselves have no meaningful relationship to language. If cat is assigned 2 and feline is assigned 6, the numerical distance between those IDs does not indicate how closely related the words are. Two sentences with similar meanings may appear numerically dissimilar when their words receive different arbitrary IDs. Integer IDs can still serve as lookup indices into an embedding table, but they should not be interpreted as measurements of meaning. The chapter uses this weakness to motivate vector spaces in which similarity can be defined more usefully.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why the numerical difference between two arbitrary vocabulary IDs cannot be interpreted as semantic distance."
Key point:
- Integer IDs identify vocabulary entries but do not encode semantic similarity.
- A learned or engineered representation is needed to express useful relationships.
Source links:
-
- Mikolov et al. (2013), Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space
20. Q: What is a vector space, and why is it useful for representing language?
A: Show answer
A vector is an ordered collection of numbers that can be treated as a point in a multidimensional space. A word or document can be represented by such a vector, allowing mathematical measures to compare representations. Unlike arbitrary integer IDs, vectors can be designed or learned so that related linguistic units occupy nearby or otherwise related positions. The chapter introduces vector spaces as a way to quantify similarity even when the number of dimensions is too large for people to visualize. A high-dimensional vector need not be intuitively interpretable to be computationally useful; algorithms can operate on its geometry.
Study instruction:
- "Represent three words as points in a simple two-dimensional illustration and explain what closeness would mean for a language task."
Key point:
- Vectors provide a mathematical representation of linguistic units.
- High-dimensional geometry can support similarity calculations without human visualization.
Source links:
-
- Mikolov et al. (2013), Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space
21. Q: How does Euclidean distance compare two vectors?
A: Show answer
Euclidean distance measures the straight-line distance between two points. For vectors A and B, it is calculated by taking the square root of the sum of squared differences between corresponding dimensions. In two dimensions this is the familiar Pythagorean distance, and the same principle extends to higher dimensions. The chapter uses the distance between points such as [0,0] and [2,1] to show how vector comparison can be computed. Euclidean distance is only one possible similarity measure. Its usefulness depends on how vectors are scaled and constructed, so a small distance is meaningful only when the representation and metric suit the task.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate the Euclidean distance between two short vectors by subtracting corresponding dimensions, squaring, summing, and taking the square root."
Key point:
- Euclidean distance compares vectors through coordinate differences.
- A distance metric is meaningful only in relation to the representation being compared.
Source links:
-
- scikit-learn User Guide, Feature Extraction and TF-IDF
22. Q: What is one-hot encoding, and how does it represent a word?
A: Show answer
One-hot encoding assigns each vocabulary item a vector whose length equals the vocabulary size. Exactly one position is set to 1 and all other positions are 0; the active position identifies the word. A sentence can be represented as a sequence of such vectors or by combining them. This makes word identity explicit and gives every vocabulary item a distinct code. However, the representation is sparse and becomes large when the vocabulary contains many entries. More importantly, it does not show that two words may be semantically related. One-hot encoding is useful for identity, but weak as a representation of linguistic meaning.
Study instruction:
- "Create one-hot vectors for four words in a four-word vocabulary and explain why each vector has one active coordinate."
Key point:
- One-hot vectors encode identity using one active coordinate.
- They are sparse and do not inherently express semantic relationships.
Source links:
-
- Mikolov et al. (2013), Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space
23. Q: Why is one-hot encoding inefficient for large vocabularies?
A: Show answer
A one-hot vector needs one coordinate for every vocabulary item even though only one coordinate is active for a particular word. As the vocabulary grows, the representation becomes longer and storage and computation can be wasteful. It is also context-free: the vector for a word is identical wherever the word appears, and it does not reveal which other words tend to occur with it. The chapter treats one-hot encoding as a useful historical step but not a sufficient final representation for language understanding. Sparse storage can reduce some memory costs, yet it does not solve the lack of semantic and contextual information.
Study instruction:
- "Estimate the number of coordinates required for a vocabulary of 50,000 words and explain why sparsity does not solve the semantic problem."
Key point:
- One-hot vector size grows with vocabulary size.
- Its central limitation is lack of semantic and contextual information.
Source links:
-
- Mikolov et al. (2013), Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space
24. Q: What is term frequency, and what does it measure?
A: Show answer
Term frequency measures how often a word occurs in a particular document. A higher count may indicate that the word is important to that document, although raw counts are affected by document length and writing style. The chapter introduces TF as one part of TF-IDF, a weighting approach that represents a document using numerical scores for its terms. Term frequency alone is insufficient because common words may appear frequently in many documents without distinguishing one document from another. It is therefore combined with inverse document frequency, which measures how widespread a term is across the collection.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate the frequency of selected terms in a short document and compare the result with a longer document on the same subject."
Key point:
- TF measures term occurrence within a document.
- Raw frequency alone does not show how distinctive a term is across a collection.
Source links:
-
- scikit-learn User Guide, Feature Extraction and TF-IDF
25. Q: What does inverse document frequency contribute to TF-IDF?
A: Show answer
Inverse document frequency reduces the weight of terms that occur in many documents and gives more weight to terms that are relatively distinctive within a collection. The chapter explains IDF through the relationship between the total number of documents and the number containing a particular term. A common word such as the carries little information for distinguishing documents, whereas a rarer term may be more informative. IDF is corpus-dependent: its value changes when the document collection changes. Implementations may use smoothing and normalization conventions, so the precise formula should be checked in the software being used.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a common term and a rare term across a document collection and explain why their IDF values differ."
Key point:
- IDF measures how widely a term is distributed across the corpus.
- A term's informativeness depends partly on the collection used to calculate it.
Source links:
-
- scikit-learn User Guide, Feature Extraction and TF-IDF
26. Q: How does TF-IDF combine term frequency and inverse document frequency?
A: Show answer
TF-IDF multiplies a term's frequency within a document by an inverse-document-frequency weight derived from the wider collection. A word that appears often in one document but rarely across the corpus receives a comparatively high score. A word that appears in nearly every document receives a lower score because it is less useful for distinguishing documents. The resulting values can form a document vector used in information retrieval, classification, or clustering. The chapter presents a conceptual formula; practical libraries may add smoothing, normalization, or alternative term-frequency scaling. Those settings should be documented because they affect numerical results.
Study instruction:
- "Compute a simple TF-IDF score for a term and explain what the TF and IDF components contribute."
Key point:
- TF-IDF combines within-document frequency with corpus-level distinctiveness.
- Implementation settings can change the exact numerical weighting.
Source links:
-
- scikit-learn User Guide, Feature Extraction and TF-IDF
27. Q: What are the strengths and limitations of TF-IDF for representing documents?
A: Show answer
TF-IDF improves on raw word IDs by assigning weights that reflect both local frequency and corpus-wide rarity. It can be effective for search, document ranking, classification, and clustering because distinctive terms influence similarity more strongly. However, it generally treats words as independent features and does not directly encode word order, syntax, or contextual meaning. Synonyms may occupy unrelated dimensions, and a word used in two different senses is usually represented by the same term feature. TF-IDF is therefore a useful sparse lexical representation, not a full model of language understanding. The chapter uses this limitation to motivate learned embeddings.
Study instruction:
- "Compare two paraphrases that use different vocabulary and explain why TF-IDF may underestimate their semantic similarity."
Key point:
- TF-IDF is useful for lexical weighting and document-level tasks.
- It does not inherently represent word order, synonymy, or context-dependent meaning.
Source links:
-
- scikit-learn User Guide, Feature Extraction and TF-IDF
28. Q: What is a word embedding?
A: Show answer
A word embedding assigns a word a dense numerical vector in a multidimensional space. Unlike one-hot encoding, an embedding can place words with similar distributional behavior near one another or in related regions. The values are learned from data rather than chosen as arbitrary identifiers. The chapter explains that embeddings preserve traces of the contexts in which words occur, allowing algorithms to use relationships among words during language tasks. Word embeddings are not dictionary definitions stored as coordinates; their meaning is distributed across learned dimensions and depends on the training data and objective.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how a learned dense vector differs from a one-hot vector and identify what the training data contribute to the embedding."
Key point:
- Embeddings are learned dense representations of words.
- Their geometry reflects patterns captured from the training data.
Source links:
-
- Mikolov et al. (2013), Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space
29. Q: What is distributional semantics, and how does Word2Vec illustrate it?
A: Show answer
Distributional semantics is the idea that linguistic units can be characterized partly by the contexts in which they occur. Word2Vec learns vectors from large text collections so that words with similar patterns of neighboring words tend to have related representations. Poor and rich, for example, are opposites in meaning but can occur in similar grammatical and topical contexts. Their distributional vectors may therefore be close even though the words are antonyms. This illustrates both the power and limitation of distributional learning: context patterns provide useful semantic signals, but similarity of usage does not always equal similarity of meaning.
Study instruction:
- "Choose two synonyms and two antonyms that occur in similar contexts, then explain how a distributional model may treat them."
Key point:
- Word2Vec learns relationships from patterns of word use.
- Distributional similarity captures context, not a complete dictionary-like account of meaning.
Source links:
-
- Mikolov et al. (2013), Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space
30. Q: How does the Cloze-test idea help explain Word2Vec training?
A: Show answer
A Cloze test asks a learner to supply a missing word using the surrounding context. The chapter uses this familiar task to explain how Word2Vec learns useful word representations: a neural network is trained either to predict a target word from context or to predict context words from a selected word. Repeated prediction makes the network adjust its parameters so that words and their typical neighbors become encoded in the learned weights. The objective is not simply to memorize a dictionary. It creates a task in which the network must capture regularities in how words appear together across a large collection of text.
Study instruction:
- "Create a masked sentence and identify the context words and the word the model is asked to predict."
Key point:
- Cloze-style prediction turns context into a learning signal.
- The prediction task causes network weights to absorb distributional information.
Source links:
-
- Mikolov et al. (2013), Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space
31. Q: What are the two standard Word2Vec architectures, CBOW and skip-gram?
A: Show answer
Word2Vec is commonly described through two training architectures. Continuous Bag of Words (CBOW) uses surrounding context words to predict the target word. Skip-gram starts with a target word and predicts nearby context words. Both use local context to learn dense word vectors, but they reverse the direction of the prediction task. The distinction matters when interpreting training diagrams because the input and target roles differ. The chapter introduces both variants through a Cloze-style explanation; the standard terminology in the Word2Vec literature is CBOW for context-to-target prediction and skip-gram for target-to-context prediction.
Study instruction:
- "Draw one sentence window and label the inputs and prediction target for CBOW and skip-gram."
Key point:
- CBOW predicts a target word from its context.
- Skip-gram predicts context words from a target word.
Source links:
-
- Mikolov et al. (2013), Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space
32. Q: How does CBOW learn from surrounding words?
A: Show answer
CBOW receives representations of context words around a missing or target position and uses them to predict the target word. In “The ___ sat on the mat,” the words around the blank constrain plausible completions. During training, the model compares its prediction with the known target and adjusts its weights. This objective encourages the learned representation to summarize information from the context window. The context is typically combined for the prediction, so the method does not preserve every aspect of word order in the way a full sequence model would. CBOW is therefore a context-to-target prediction task.
Study instruction:
- "Take a short sentence, hide its central word, and list which neighboring words CBOW uses to predict it."
Key point:
- CBOW uses surrounding words as input to predict the target.
- Training adjusts parameters to improve contextually appropriate predictions.
Source links:
-
- Mikolov et al. (2013), Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space
33. Q: How does skip-gram learn from a target word?
A: Show answer
Skip-gram takes a target word as input and trains the model to predict words that occur within a selected context window around it. If the target is sat, nearby words such as the, on, and mat may become prediction targets depending on the window definition. Repeating this task across a large corpus encourages the model to learn a vector that captures regularities in the target word's surrounding contexts. Window size is a design choice: a narrow window emphasizes local relationships, while a wider window can capture broader topical associations. Skip-gram therefore learns by predicting context from a central word.
Study instruction:
- "Use one target word and two different context-window sizes to show how the prediction targets change."
Key point:
- Skip-gram predicts neighboring context words from a target word.
- The context-window size influences which relationships the model learns.
Source links:
-
- Mikolov et al. (2013), Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space
34. Q: How does Word2Vec use a neural network to learn word vectors?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a network that receives a one-hot representation and maps it through a hidden layer to predict context-related words. Training repeatedly adjusts the network weights when the predicted context differs from the observed context. In the illustrative architecture, a vocabulary-sized input connects to a smaller hidden layer, producing a large weight matrix. Once training is complete, the learned weights associated with each word can be extracted as its vector representation. The hidden units need not be individually interpretable; their purpose is to support the prediction task and encode useful distributional regularities.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how a one-hot input can select a row of a learned weight matrix and how training changes that row."
Key point:
- Word2Vec learns embeddings as part of a prediction network.
- The trained input-to-hidden weights can be used as word vectors.
Source links:
-
- Mikolov et al. (2013), Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space
35. Q: Why can a Word2Vec embedding matrix be used as a vocabulary of learned vectors?
A: Show answer
In the chapter's example, each vocabulary item is represented by a one-hot input connected to the hidden layer. The corresponding row of the input-to-hidden weight matrix contains the learned values associated with that word. After training, that row can be retrieved as the word's dense vector. The vector is useful because the training objective has adjusted its values to support predictions about neighboring words. It is not a manually labelled feature list, and individual coordinates usually have no simple human-readable meaning. The embedding is useful through its relationships with other vectors and through the task for which it was learned.
Study instruction:
- "Describe how a vocabulary index selects a vector from an embedding matrix and why its coordinates are not normally interpreted individually."
Key point:
- An embedding matrix stores one learned vector for each vocabulary entry.
- The usefulness of a vector lies in learned relationships, not necessarily interpretable dimensions.
Source links:
-
- Mikolov et al. (2013), Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space
36. Q: Why can the vectors for poor and rich be close even though the words are opposites?
A: Show answer
Word2Vec learns from distributional patterns, so it may place words close together when they appear in similar contexts. Poor and rich can both modify the same kinds of nouns and occur in similar sentence structures, even though their meanings contrast. The vector therefore reflects patterns of use rather than a simple positive-to-negative meaning scale. This shows that distributional similarity can capture syntactic and topical relationships while failing to distinguish synonymy from antonymy. The result depends on the training corpus, context window, and objective, so vector neighborhoods should be interpreted as evidence about usage patterns rather than definitive semantic categories.
Study instruction:
- "Find an antonym pair that appears in similar grammatical contexts and explain why a distributional model might place the words near each other."
Key point:
- Similar contexts can produce similar vectors even for antonyms.
- Distributional proximity is not identical to synonymy or semantic equivalence.
Source links:
-
- Mikolov et al. (2013), Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space
37. Q: Why do static Word2Vec vectors struggle with homonyms?
A: Show answer
A static Word2Vec model generally assigns one learned vector to a vocabulary word regardless of the context in which it appears. A homonym such as bank can refer to a financial institution or the side of a river. If both senses occur in the training corpus, the single vector may combine information from both contexts. The result can be an overloaded representation that is not fully appropriate for either meaning. The chapter uses this problem to motivate contextual representations. A model that adjusts a token representation according to the surrounding sentence can distinguish uses more effectively than one fixed vector.
Study instruction:
- "Compare “deposit money at the bank” with “sit on the river bank” and explain why one static vector may mix the two senses."
Key point:
- Static embeddings provide one vector per vocabulary word.
- Context-dependent meanings motivate contextualized representations.
Source links:
-
- Mikolov et al. (2013), Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space
- Devlin et al. (2018), BERT: Pre-training of Deep Bidirectional Transformers for Language Understanding
38. Q: What problem did the Transformer architecture address?
A: Show answer
The Transformer was introduced as an alternative to sequence-to-sequence models built around recurrent or convolutional processing. The 2017 paper proposed an architecture based on attention mechanisms rather than recurrence or convolution. This allowed the model to relate positions in a sequence through attention computations and made important parts of training more parallelizable. The chapter presents Transformers as the architectural foundation underlying modern LLMs. Their significance is not simply that they are larger neural networks; they change how information from different positions is combined to build representations for language tasks.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the architectural shift introduced by Transformers and explain why removing recurrence changed sequence processing."
Key point:
- Transformers rely on attention rather than recurrent or convolutional sequence processing.
- The architecture made sequence modelling more parallelizable during training.
Source links:
-
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
39. Q: What is the role of the encoder in an encoder-decoder Transformer?
A: Show answer
The encoder processes the source sequence and produces contextual vector representations. Through its layers and attention operations, it allows each position to incorporate information from other relevant positions in the input. These representations form the information that the decoder uses when generating a target sequence. In machine translation, for example, the encoder processes an English sentence and produces representations that support the generation of a Dutch translation. The encoder does not itself need to produce the final target sentence; its role is to create a context-rich representation of the input for the rest of the model.
Study instruction:
- "Trace how an input sentence passes through an encoder and describe what information its output representations should preserve."
Key point:
- The encoder transforms source text into contextual representations.
- Those representations provide information used by the decoder.
Source links:
-
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
40. Q: What is the role of the decoder in an encoder-decoder Transformer?
A: Show answer
The decoder generates a target sequence using the encoder's representations together with the target tokens generated or supplied so far. During training, the decoder can receive the correct preceding target tokens, shifted so that it predicts the next position. During inference, the correct future target is not available, so the decoder conditions on its own previous outputs. The chapter explains this as a word-by-word process in which each new output becomes part of the context for the next prediction. The decoder converts encoded source information and a growing target history into a sequence of outputs.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a translation example and distinguish source information supplied by the encoder from the target history used by the decoder."
Key point:
- The decoder generates the target sequence step by step.
- It uses encoded source information and preceding target context.
Source links:
-
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
41. Q: Why are training and inference different for a sequence-to-sequence decoder?
A: Show answer
During training, the model can be given the known target sequence so that a prediction at one position is based on the correct previous target tokens. This arrangement is often called teacher forcing and is represented in the chapter by right-shifted decoder input. During inference, the target sequence is not known in advance. The decoder begins with a start symbol and then uses its own generated outputs as context for subsequent predictions. Errors can therefore influence later steps in a way that does not occur in the same form when training uses correct previous tokens. This distinction matters when evaluating model behavior outside training.
Study instruction:
- "Write the shifted decoder inputs for a four-token target sentence and compare them with the sequence available at inference."
Key point:
- Training can use known previous target tokens as inputs.
- Inference must condition on the model's own generated history.
Source links:
-
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
42. Q: What is attention in a Transformer, and why is it important?
A: Show answer
Attention is a mechanism for weighting information from different positions so that a representation can use the parts of the sequence most relevant to its current computation. The chapter introduces it through a metaphor in which words attend to other words with which they are associated. In the architecture, learned transformations produce query, key, and value representations that determine how information is weighted and combined. Attention helps a token representation incorporate context from other positions rather than relying only on a fixed local window or one previous state. It is central to the Transformer's ability to build contextual representations.
Study instruction:
- "Take a sentence with an ambiguous word and identify which surrounding words an attention mechanism might need to use."
Key point:
- Attention weights information from other sequence positions.
- It allows token representations to incorporate relevant context from the input.
Source links:
-
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
43. Q: What are queries, keys, and values in self-attention?
A: Show answer
In self-attention, each token representation is transformed into query, key, and value vectors. Queries express what a position is looking for; keys provide representations against which queries are compared; values carry the information that will be combined according to the resulting attention weights. Similarity scores between queries and keys are converted into weights, and those weights determine how strongly each value contributes to the updated representation. These are computational roles, not literal questions or labels stored in the words. The chapter uses the query-key-value process to explain how learned attention connects token representations across a sequence.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the three roles using a sentence in which bank must attend to money-related or river-related context."
Key point:
- Queries and keys determine attention weights.
- Values carry the information combined using those weights.
Source links:
-
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
44. Q: How does multi-head attention extend a single attention operation?
A: Show answer
Multi-head attention runs several attention computations in parallel, each using its own learned projections. Different heads can therefore form different patterns of relationships among positions. Their outputs are combined to create a representation that brings together information from multiple attention subspaces. The chapter gives an illustrative example in which a 512-dimensional representation is divided across eight heads, with each head operating on a smaller vector. Those numbers are examples, not universal settings for all Transformer models. The benefit is that the network can learn several complementary ways of relating sequence elements in the same layer.
Study instruction:
- "Sketch four parallel attention heads and explain why their outputs are combined rather than treated as four separate final answers."
Key point:
- Multiple heads allow parallel attention patterns.
- Head count and dimensionality are architecture choices, not fixed constants.
Source links:
-
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
45. Q: How do stacked Transformer layers refine token representations?
A: Show answer
A Transformer typically contains repeated layers that process the output representations of earlier layers. Each layer applies attention and additional transformations so that information can be recombined and refined over successive stages. In the chapter's example, several encoder layers are stacked, with each layer receiving the representation produced by the previous one. The resulting vector can carry information from a wider range of relationships than the initial token representation. The exact number of layers and heads depends on model design. More layers increase capacity and computational cost, and do not automatically guarantee better performance without suitable training and data.
Study instruction:
- "Trace one token representation through three stacked Transformer layers and explain what information can be added at each stage."
Key point:
- Transformer layers repeatedly update contextual representations.
- Depth and head count are design choices with computational trade-offs.
Source links:
-
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
46. Q: Why does the chapter say Transformer representations are task-dependent?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that attention patterns and learned representations are shaped by the task used to train the model. A Transformer trained for translation may learn to connect words differently from one trained for dialogue or another language task. The training objective determines which relationships are useful for reducing prediction error. Consequently, the same architecture can develop different internal representations when trained on different data and objectives. Architecture alone does not determine behavior: the training corpus, objective, parameterization, and later adaptation all matter. A model's learned representations should be understood in relation to the task it was optimized to perform.
Study instruction:
- "Compare how a translation model and a dialogue model might need different relationships between the same words."
Key point:
- Representations are shaped by training objectives and data.
- The Transformer architecture alone does not determine model behavior.
Source links:
-
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
47. Q: What is BERT, and how does it use the Transformer architecture?
A: Show answer
BERT stands for Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers. It uses the encoder part of the Transformer to produce contextual representations of text. Unlike a decoder that generates left-to-right, BERT can use context from both the left and right sides of a masked token during its pretraining objective. The chapter presents BERT as a way to improve on static word embeddings because a token representation can be adapted to the sentence in which it appears. BERT is therefore an encoder-based language representation model that can be adapted to tasks such as question answering or named-entity recognition.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why an encoder-only architecture is suitable for contextual representation tasks such as classification or extraction."
Key point:
- BERT uses Transformer encoders to build bidirectional contextual representations.
- Its primary purpose is language representation, not ordinary left-to-right generation.
Source links:
-
- Devlin et al. (2018), BERT: Pre-training of Deep Bidirectional Transformers for Language Understanding
48. Q: What pretraining objectives did the original BERT use?
A: Show answer
The original BERT paper introduced masked language modelling and next-sentence prediction. For masked language modelling, selected tokens are hidden and the model learns to recover them using surrounding context. The next-sentence objective asks the model to predict whether a second sentence follows the first in the training example. Together, these objectives encourage the encoder to represent local token context and relationships across sentence pairs. The chapter describes these as BERT's two training purposes. Later systems and BERT variants have changed or removed particular objectives, so the original design should not be assumed to describe every model bearing the BERT name.
Study instruction:
- "Create one masked-token example and one sentence-pair example, then state what the original BERT objectives ask the model to predict."
Key point:
- Masked language modelling trains BERT to recover hidden tokens from context.
- Original BERT also used a next-sentence-prediction objective.
Source links:
-
- Devlin et al. (2018), BERT: Pre-training of Deep Bidirectional Transformers for Language Understanding
49. Q: How does BERT create contextual representations for ambiguous words?
A: Show answer
BERT begins with pretrained token representations and then uses attention to adjust them according to the surrounding sentence. The chapter uses bank as an example: in a sentence about deposits, nearby words indicate a financial meaning; in a sentence about a river, context points to a geographic meaning. The same vocabulary item can therefore receive different contextual representations in different sentences. This is a major contrast with static Word2Vec vectors, which generally assign one vector to a word type. Context-sensitive representation does not guarantee perfect disambiguation, but it gives downstream tasks richer information about the actual use of a token.
Study instruction:
- "Compare two sentences containing bank and explain which contextual words should influence the token representation in each case."
Key point:
- BERT adjusts token representations using surrounding context.
- Contextual embeddings address an important limitation of static word vectors.
Source links:
-
- Devlin et al. (2018), BERT: Pre-training of Deep Bidirectional Transformers for Language Understanding
- Mikolov et al. (2013), Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space
50. Q: What are the [CLS] and [SEP] tokens used for in the chapter's BERT description?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that [SEP] tokens mark sentence boundaries in BERT's input format, while [CLS] is a special token associated with representing information from the full input for sentence-level tasks such as the original next-sentence objective. These are not ordinary vocabulary words in the sentence. They provide structural signals that help the model organize paired inputs and support task-specific outputs. Their precise use depends on the BERT implementation and downstream task, so they should be understood as special boundary or control tokens rather than words with ordinary lexical meaning.
Study instruction:
- "Mark sentence boundaries in a paired BERT input and explain why special tokens are needed in addition to ordinary words."
Key point:
- [SEP] marks input-segment boundaries in the described format.
- [CLS] provides a sequence-level representation used by certain BERT tasks.
Source links:
-
- Devlin et al. (2018), BERT: Pre-training of Deep Bidirectional Transformers for Language Understanding
51. Q: How can a pretrained BERT model be adapted to a downstream task?
A: Show answer
After pretraining, BERT contains contextual representations and learned Transformer parameters that can be reused. A task-specific output layer can be added and the model fine-tuned using labelled examples for a particular application. The chapter gives named-entity recognition as an example: the pretrained model is adapted so its representations support identifying names or other entity categories. Fine-tuning uses task data to adjust the model to the new objective. This is more efficient than learning all language representations from scratch, although adaptation quality still depends on task data, labels, evaluation, and controls against overfitting.
Study instruction:
- "Design a fine-tuning plan for named-entity recognition, identifying input text, labels, output layer, and evaluation."
Key point:
- Pretrained BERT representations can be adapted to downstream tasks.
- Fine-tuning aligns the model with task-specific labels and objectives.
Source links:
-
- Devlin et al. (2018), BERT: Pre-training of Deep Bidirectional Transformers for Language Understanding
52. Q: What is a decoder-only Transformer, and how does it differ from BERT?
A: Show answer
A decoder-only Transformer generates each next token using the left context already available. It does not use a separate bidirectional encoder to create representations from both sides of the input. BERT, by contrast, uses an encoder to build bidirectional contextual representations for understanding-oriented tasks. A decoder-only model is autoregressive: each new prediction is conditioned on preceding tokens and the model's internal representation of that history. The chapter notes that many current LLMs use decoder-only architectures because this structure directly supports next-token generation. The distinction concerns information flow and task orientation, not simply model size.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the direction of context available to BERT and a decoder-only model when processing a sentence."
Key point:
- BERT is encoder-based and uses bidirectional context for representation learning.
- Decoder-only Transformers generate from left context in an autoregressive process.
Source links:
-
- Devlin et al. (2018), BERT: Pre-training of Deep Bidirectional Transformers for Language Understanding
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
53. Q: What does autoregressive generation mean in a decoder-only LLM?
A: Show answer
Autoregressive generation means that the model predicts the next token from the tokens already generated or supplied. It begins with a prompt, computes a probability distribution over possible next tokens, selects one according to its decoding procedure, and appends it to the context. The process repeats, with each new token influencing the next prediction. The chapter describes this as generating a sentence word by word, but modern models generally operate on tokens that may be whole words, word pieces, punctuation, or other units. Generation ends when a stopping condition is met, such as an end token or length limit.
Study instruction:
- "Trace a five-step generation sequence and show how the context changes after each newly produced token."
Key point:
- Autoregressive models condition each prediction on preceding context.
- The generated unit is a token, which is not always a whole word.
Source links:
-
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
54. Q: How does a prompt prime a decoder-only language model?
A: Show answer
A prompt supplies the initial context from which a decoder-only model begins predicting. The model processes the prompt tokens and uses their contextual representations to estimate which token should come next. After the first token is generated, that token becomes part of the context for the next prediction. The chapter illustrates this with a partial sentence such as “Large Language Models ...” and shows how the continuation depends on preceding words. A prompt does not provide a complete answer that the model merely retrieves; it conditions a sequence of probabilistic next-token decisions. The continuation is influenced by the prompt, model parameters, and decoding settings.
Study instruction:
- "Write two different prompts for the same completion task and explain how each changes the context for next-token prediction."
Key point:
- A prompt establishes the initial left context for generation.
- Each generated token becomes part of the context for subsequent predictions.
Source links:
-
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
55. Q: How should the chapter's statement that most current LLMs are decoder-only be interpreted?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses decoder-only Transformers to explain the architecture common among many prominent generative language models. This should not be read as saying that every language model or every system called an LLM has the same architecture. Encoder-only models such as BERT are useful for contextual representation and understanding tasks, while encoder-decoder models remain useful for sequence-to-sequence applications. Architecture choice depends on the task, training objective, and deployment requirements. The safest interpretation is that decoder-only models are a major and widely used design for open-ended text generation, not the only possible architecture for language processing.
Study instruction:
- "Classify a masked-token model, a translation model, and an open-ended text generator by likely architecture and explain the task fit."
Key point:
- Decoder-only Transformers are widely used for generative language modelling.
- Encoder-only and encoder-decoder designs remain useful for other language tasks.
Source links:
-
- Devlin et al. (2018), BERT: Pre-training of Deep Bidirectional Transformers for Language Understanding
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
56. Q: What is the difference between static embeddings, contextual embeddings, and decoder hidden states?
A: Show answer
Static embeddings assign a relatively fixed vector to a vocabulary item, as in standard Word2Vec. Contextual embeddings adjust a token representation according to surrounding words, as BERT does through bidirectional attention. Decoder hidden states in an autoregressive model are conditioned on the preceding context available at each generation step. All are vector representations, but they differ in the context they can use and the objective that shaped them. Treating them as interchangeable obscures important architectural differences. A system designer should identify whether a representation is fixed by word type, contextualized by both sides, or conditioned on left context for generation.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the context available to Word2Vec, BERT, and decoder-only representations in three columns."
Key point:
- Representation type determines which context can influence a vector.
- Static, bidirectional contextual, and autoregressive representations serve different purposes.
Source links:
-
- Mikolov et al. (2013), Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space
- Devlin et al. (2018), BERT: Pre-training of Deep Bidirectional Transformers for Language Understanding
57. Q: What is the relationship between embeddings and an LLM's ability to generate language?
A: Show answer
Embeddings provide numerical representations that allow neural networks to process and relate tokens. Word2Vec demonstrated how a prediction task can produce vectors containing information about distributional context. Transformer-based models extend this idea by repeatedly updating token representations through attention and learned transformations. In a generative model, these contextual states help estimate the probability of the next token. The chapter's idea that embeddings are both a by-product and fuel for LLMs captures this relationship: training to predict language shapes internal representations, and those representations support later predictions. Embeddings alone are not a complete language model; architecture and training objective are also required.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how token embeddings enter a Transformer and how contextual states contribute to next-token prediction."
Key point:
- Training objectives shape useful internal representations.
- Embeddings are part of a larger architecture and do not alone constitute an LLM.
Source links:
-
- Mikolov et al. (2013), Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
58. Q: What common misconceptions should be avoided when studying the chapter's architecture history?
A: Show answer
A perceptron's failure on XOR does not mean all neural networks fail on XOR; hidden layers and nonlinearities change what a network can represent. One-hot vectors identify words but do not encode semantic relationships. TF-IDF weights lexical features but does not fully represent meaning. Word2Vec vectors reflect distributional patterns and can place antonyms near each other. Attention is not simply a count of co-occurrences; it is computed from learned query, key, and value representations. BERT's bidirectional encoder and a decoder-only generator also have different objectives and context directions. These distinctions prevent misleading comparisons between architectures and representations.
Study instruction:
- "Write a corrected explanation for each misconception and give an example that demonstrates the difference."
Key point:
- Architecture and representation choices determine what a model can learn.
- Historical explanations should not be treated as universal descriptions of every modern implementation.
Source links:
-
- MIT Press, Minsky and Papert, Perceptrons
- scikit-learn User Guide, Feature Extraction and TF-IDF
- Mikolov et al. (2013), Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
- Devlin et al. (2018), BERT: Pre-training of Deep Bidirectional Transformers for Language Understanding
59. Q: How can the chapter's progression be summarized as a single technical pipeline?
A: Show answer
The progression begins with simple neuron-like units that transform numerical inputs into outputs. The perceptron adds learnable weights but is restricted to linear decision boundaries. Multilayer networks add hidden layers and nonlinear transformations, enabling richer representations. Language processing then requires words to be encoded numerically. One-hot vectors identify words, TF-IDF weights document terms, and Word2Vec learns dense vectors from context-prediction tasks. Transformers use attention to combine contextual information across positions. BERT uses encoder-based bidirectional representations, while decoder-only models use left context to predict tokens autoregressively. Each stage addresses a limitation in an earlier representation or architecture.
Study instruction:
- "Reconstruct the progression from perceptron to decoder-only LLM and annotate the problem each major step addresses."
Key point:
- The chapter is a sequence of solutions to representation and architecture limitations.
- Modern LLMs combine deep networks, contextual vectors, attention, and next-token prediction.
Source links:
-
- McCulloch and Pitts (1943), A Logical Calculus of the Ideas Immanent in Nervous Activity
- Mikolov et al. (2013), Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
- Devlin et al. (2018), BERT: Pre-training of Deep Bidirectional Transformers for Language Understanding
60. Q: How should a practitioner choose between TF-IDF, Word2Vec, BERT, and a decoder-only LLM?
A: Show answer
The choice should begin with the task, data, and performance requirements. TF-IDF can be effective for search, ranking, or simple classification when distinctive terms are informative and low computational cost matters. Word2Vec provides dense static vectors useful for similarity or features when distributional relationships are sufficient. BERT can provide contextual representations for classification, extraction, and other understanding tasks. A decoder-only LLM is suitable when the system must generate a continuation or response conditioned on a prompt. These options are not interchangeable upgrades: each has different context access, training needs, cost, and failure modes. Evaluation should use representative data and task-specific measures.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one practical text task and justify a representation or architecture using context needs, cost, and output requirements."
Key point:
- Choose representations and architectures according to the task rather than popularity.
- Evaluation must reflect the actual use case and operational constraints.
Source links:
-
- scikit-learn User Guide, Feature Extraction and TF-IDF
- Mikolov et al. (2013), Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space
- Devlin et al. (2018), BERT: Pre-training of Deep Bidirectional Transformers for Language Understanding
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
61. Q: What is the chapter's most important lesson about the relationship between architecture and learning objectives?
A: Show answer
An architecture defines how information can flow and be transformed, while the learning objective determines what the model is trained to achieve. A perceptron learns a boundary from labelled examples; Word2Vec learns vectors through context prediction; BERT learns contextual representations through masked-token and sentence-pair objectives; and a decoder-only model learns to predict the next token from preceding context. The same broad family of neural components can therefore produce different internal representations when trained for different objectives. Understanding an LLM requires examining both its structure and the task that shaped its parameters, not merely counting layers or parameters.
Study instruction:
- "For each model in the chapter, record its architecture, training signal, and intended output in separate columns."
Key point:
- Architecture controls information flow; objectives shape what is learned.
- Model behavior cannot be explained by architecture alone.
Source links:
-
- Rosenblatt (1958), The Perceptron: A Probabilistic Model for Information Storage and Organization in the Brain
- Mikolov et al. (2013), Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space
- Devlin et al. (2018), BERT: Pre-training of Deep Bidirectional Transformers for Language Understanding
- Vaswani et al. (2017), Attention Is All You Need
Rows: 61
Table 4
Human-Guided Adaptation of Large Language Models: Prompting, Fine-Tuning, and Preference Optimization
1. Q: What is the central purpose of Chapter 4, “Learning from Humans”?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains how a pretrained language model can be developed into a system that follows instructions and produces responses more aligned with human preferences. It distinguishes base or foundation models, instruction-fine-tuned models, and assistant-style systems optimized with human feedback. It then examines continued pretraining, in-context learning, zero-shot and few-shot prompting, supervised fine-tuning, parameter-efficient adaptation, quantization, and reinforcement learning from human feedback. The central theme is that model capability is shaped not only by architecture and pretraining but also by the examples, instructions, feedback, and optimization objectives used after pretraining.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the chapter as a progression from broad language pretraining to instruction learning and preference optimization."
Key point:
- Human-provided instructions and feedback change how a pretrained model behaves.
- Different adaptation stages add different capabilities and costs.
2. Q: What three broad manifestations of LLMs does the chapter distinguish?
A: Show answer
The chapter distinguishes base or foundation models, fine-tuned models, and assistant-style models optimized with human feedback. A base model learns broad language patterns from large text collections but may not reliably follow instructions. A fine-tuned model receives examples of instructions and desired answers, improving its ability to respond to requests. An assistant-style model receives further optimization based on human evaluations or preferences, with the aim of improving response quality and usefulness. These are stages in a development spectrum, not mutually exclusive product categories in every deployment.
Study instruction:
- "Create a comparison table showing the training additions, typical behavior, and remaining limitations of each of the three model stages."
Key point:
- Base pretraining, supervised instruction tuning, and preference optimization serve different purposes.
- The stages build on one another but are not identical.
3. Q: What is a foundation model in the chapter's discussion?
A: Show answer
A foundation model is a model trained on broad data, generally through large-scale self-supervision, that can be adapted to a range of downstream tasks. The chapter uses this idea to describe a broadly pretrained model before it has been specialized for a particular instruction-following role. Its broad exposure provides reusable language capabilities, but it does not guarantee that the model will obey requests, follow a desired response format, or optimize for human preferences. Adaptation is what turns broad pretrained capability into more task-directed behavior.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish broad pretraining from later adaptation and identify at least two downstream tasks that could use the same foundation model."
Key point:
- A foundation model is broadly pretrained and adaptable.
- Broad language knowledge does not automatically provide reliable instruction following.
4. Q: What is a base model, and what does it learn during pretraining?
A: Show answer
A base model is the pretrained form of an LLM before instruction fine-tuning or preference optimization. It learns statistical regularities in large collections of text, including word co-occurrences, syntax, discourse patterns, and ways that sequences tend to continue. The chapter describes this as the model's initial language-learning stage. The model may generate fluent text because it has learned plausible continuations, but its objective does not necessarily teach it to interpret every imperative as a task request. It can therefore be capable at language generation while still being unreliable as an assistant.
Study instruction:
- "Explain what a next-token or language-prediction objective teaches and what it does not necessarily teach about following user instructions."
Key point:
- Pretraining develops broad language patterns.
- A base model's continuation ability is not the same as assistant behavior.
5. Q: Why might a base model fail to follow a direct instruction such as “Write a poem”?
A: Show answer
A base model is trained primarily to continue text according to patterns in its training data. If it has not been explicitly trained on instruction-response examples, it may treat an imperative sentence as text to be continued rather than as a command to perform a task. The chapter illustrates this with a prompt that can lead to a plausible continuation about a teacher rather than an actual poem. This is not necessarily a failure of grammar or fluency; it is a mismatch between the model's pretraining objective and the user's expectation of instruction following.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a continuation-style prompt and an instruction-response example, and explain what training experience would help the model distinguish them."
Key point:
- Base models optimize plausible continuation rather than guaranteed task obedience.
- Instruction-response training teaches a more useful interaction pattern.
6. Q: What is supervised instruction fine-tuning?
A: Show answer
Supervised instruction fine-tuning trains a pretrained model on examples that pair instructions with desired responses. The model's parameters are updated so that it becomes more likely to produce answers in the requested form when it encounters similar or related instructions. The chapter presents this as instruction learning: the model learns from human-prepared examples rather than relying only on broad language continuation. A sufficiently capable model can generalize from these examples to new requests, although performance depends on the quality, coverage, and clarity of the fine-tuning data.
Study instruction:
- "Design a small instruction-response dataset for one task and identify the input text, target answer, and training purpose."
Key point:
- Supervised fine-tuning updates model weights using instruction-answer examples.
- The aim is to improve generalization to new instructions, not just memorize examples.
7. Q: What is meant by instruction learning?
A: Show answer
Instruction learning is the process of teaching a model to interpret task descriptions and respond according to them. The chapter uses instruction-response examples to show how a base model can learn that a request such as writing a poem should be followed by a poem rather than by an arbitrary continuation. Instruction learning can occur through temporary examples supplied in a conversation or through training that updates the model's parameters. The essential target is the mapping from a task instruction to a suitable response, not simply the production of grammatical text.
Study instruction:
- "For a task, write several differently worded instructions that should lead to the same kind of response and explain what generalization the model should learn."
Key point:
- Instruction learning teaches the relationship between requests and appropriate outputs.
- It can be temporary through context or persistent through weight updates.
8. Q: What is in-context learning?
A: Show answer
In-context learning is the ability of an LLM to condition its response on instructions and examples supplied in the current prompt or conversation. The chapter describes it as learning during runtime without changing the model's underlying weights. The model uses the content held in its context buffer to infer the task and the desired response pattern. This can allow a base model to perform a new task from a small number of examples, provided it already has sufficient underlying capability. In-context learning is therefore a form of temporary task adaptation rather than permanent retraining.
Study instruction:
- "Give a model a task description and two demonstrations, then identify which information is provided in the prompt and which knowledge must already be in the model."
Key point:
- In-context learning adapts behavior using the current prompt.
- The model's parameters are not updated by ordinary prompting.
9. Q: How does in-context learning differ from persistent fine-tuning?
A: Show answer
In-context learning places instructions and examples in the active context so they can influence the current interaction. When that context is removed or the conversation ends, the model's weights remain unchanged and the temporary guidance is not stored as a permanent parameter update. Fine-tuning, by contrast, trains the model on additional examples and changes its parameters. The chapter compares in-context learning to updating working memory and fine-tuning to changing persistent storage. The distinction matters for cost, repeatability, privacy, deployment, and whether a behavior should remain available across future sessions.
Study instruction:
- "Classify an adaptation request as temporary context learning or persistent weight adaptation and justify the choice."
Key point:
- Prompt context is temporary working memory.
- Fine-tuning changes model parameters and can make behavior persist.
10. Q: What is a context buffer, and why is it important for in-context learning?
A: Show answer
A context buffer is the portion of memory used to hold the current input, instructions, examples, and conversational history.
